3.2.
The Chase for Generalised SO Dependencies

A standard way to answer the query
\mathsf{Q}
over a dependency
\Sigma
and a
base instance
B
is to compute a universal model for
\Sigma
and
B
using an
appropriate variant of the chase algorithm, and then to simply ‘read off’ the
facts that use the
\mathsf{Q}
predicate and consist of constants only. Numerous
chase variants have been proposed
(
Maier et al., 1979
;
Beeri and Vardi, 1984
;
Johnson and Klug, 1984
;
Fagin et al., 2005a
;
Calì et al., 2008
;
Marnette, 2009
;
ten Cate et al., 2009
;
Mecca et al., 2012
;
Deutsch et al., 2008
;
Fagin et al., 2005b
;
Arenas et al., 2011
)
. To the best of our knowledge,

Arenas et al. (2011)
presented the first chase variant that
is applicable to second-order dependencies with equality atoms in the heads.
Our key results are largely independent of the details of the chase procedure:
all that matters is that a suitable chase procedure exists and terminates on

\Sigma
and
B
. Nevertheless, to frame this paper properly, we next
recapitulate the chase variant by
Arenas et al. (2011)
,
which we extend in the obvious way to handle both first- and second-order
quantification. As we discuss later, distinguishing first- and second-order
quantification will provide opportunities for optimisation that would be lost
if we simply converted all first-order quantifiers into second-order ones. The
proof that this chase variant produces a universal model is a straightforward
variation of the proof by
Arenas et al. (2011)
, so we do
not discuss it any further.

The chase algorithm takes as input a generalised second-order dependency

\Sigma
and a base instance
B
. The algorithm uses
labelled
nulls
—objects whose existence is implied by first- and second-order
quantifiers. We distinguish a countably infinite set of
base labelled
nulls
disjoint with the set of constants, and a countably infinite set of

functional labelled nulls
defined inductively as the smallest set
containing a distinct object
\mathsf{n}_{t}
for each term of the form
{t=f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})}
where
f
is a function variable and
{u_{1},\dots,u_{n}}
are
constants or (base or functional) labelled nulls. Intuitively, base and
functional labelled nulls will be used to satisfy first-order and second-order
quantifiers, respectively. Furthermore, we assume that all constants and
labelled nulls are totally ordered using an arbitrary, but fixed ordering

\prec
, where all constants precede all labelled nulls. Finally, for each

n
-ary function variable
f
, the algorithm introduces a distinct fresh

n+1
-ary predicate
\mathsf{V}_{f}
.

We next specify how to evaluate a term in a set of facts. To this end, let
I

be an instance consisting of facts constructed using the predicates of
\Sigma

and
B
, the equality predicate
\approx
, and the predicates
\mathsf{V}_{f}
, and
furthermore assume that
{\{\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v),\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v^{\prime})\}\subseteq I}
implies
{v=v^{\prime}}
for all
f
and

{u_{1},\dots,u_{n}}
. For
t
a term of depth at most one, we define the value of

t
in
I
, written
t^{I}
, as follows.

•

For
t
a constant or a (base or functional) labelled null, we define

{t^{I}=t}
.

•

For
{t=f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})}
, we define
{t^{I}=v}
if there exists a
fact
\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v)\in I
, and we define
{t^{I}=\mathsf{n}_{t}}

if
I
does not contain a fact of the form
\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v)
.

Moreover, let
{F=R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})}
be an equality or a relational ground
atom where terms
{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}}
are all of depth at most one. We specify
whether
F
is satisfied in
I
, written
{I\vdash F}
, as follows.

•

If
F
is an equality
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
, then
{I\vdash t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
if
{t_{1}^{I}=t_{2}^{I}}
.

•

Otherwise,
{I\vdash R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})}
if
{R(t_{1}^{I},\dots,t_{n}^{I})\in I}
.

Given
\Sigma
and
B
, the chase algorithm constructs a sequence of pairs

{\langle I^{0},\mu^{0}\rangle,\langle I^{1},\mu^{1}\rangle,\dots}
. Each
I^{i}
is
an instance containing facts constructed using constants and labelled nulls,
and using the predicates of
\Sigma
and
B
, the equality predicate
\approx
,
and the predicates
\mathsf{V}_{f}
. Each
\mu^{i}
is a mapping from constants to
constants, and it will record constant representatives. For
\alpha
an atom,

\mu^{i}(\alpha)
is the result of replacing each occurrence of a constant
c
in

\alpha
on which
\mu^{i}
is defined with
\mu^{i}(c)
; note that
c
is replaced
even if it occurs as a proper subterm of an argument of
\alpha
. Moreover, for

\alpha
a conjunction,
\mu^{i}(\alpha)
is the result of applying
\mu^{i}
to
each conjunct of
\alpha
. The algorithm initialises
I^{0}
to
B
and the
mapping
\mu^{0}
to the identity on all the constants occurring in
I^{0}
and

\Sigma
. For
{i\geq 0}
, a pair
{\langle I^{i+1},\mu^{i+1}\rangle}
is
obtained from
{\langle I^{i},\mu^{i}\rangle}
by applying one of the following
two steps.

To apply an
equality step
, choose an equality
{s_{1}\approx s_{2}\in I^{i}}
. Let
{t_{1}=s_{1}}
and
{t_{2}=s_{2}}
if
{s_{1}\preceq s_{2}}
, and let

{t_{1}=s_{2}}
and
{t_{2}=s_{1}}
otherwise. Let
I^{i+1}_{a}
be the instance
obtained from
{I^{i}\setminus\{s_{1}\approx s_{2}\}}
by replacing
t_{2}
with

t_{1}
, and let
I^{i+1}
and
\mu^{i+1}(c)
for each constant
c
in the domain
of
\mu^{i}
be defined as follows.

\displaystyle I^{i+1}

\displaystyle=I^{i+1}_{a}\cup\Big\{v\approx v^{\prime}\mid v\neq v^{\prime}\text{ and }\exists u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\text{ s.t. }\{\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v),\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v^{\prime})\}\subseteq I^{i+1}_{a}\Big\}

\displaystyle\mu^{i+1}(c)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t_{1}&\text{if }\mu^{i}(c)=t_{2},\\
\mu^{i}(c)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

To apply a
dependency step
, choose a conjunct
{\forall\mathbf{x}.\big[\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]}
of

\Sigma
and a substitution
\sigma
such that
{I^{i}\vdash\mu^{i}(\varphi(\mathbf{x})\sigma)}
, and
{I^{i}\not\vdash\mu^{i}(\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sigma^{\prime})}
for each
substitution
\sigma^{\prime}
that extends
\sigma
by mapping the variables
\mathbf{y}

to the terms of
I^{i}
. Let
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
be the substitution that extends
\sigma

by mapping the variables
\mathbf{y}
to fresh base labelled nulls not occurring in

I^{i}
. Furthermore, let
I^{i+1}_{a}
be
I^{i}
extended as follows: for each term
of the form
{t=f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})}
that occurs as an argument of an atom in

\mu^{i}(\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sigma^{\prime\prime})
such that
I^{i}
does not contain a fact
of the form
\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v)
, add the fact

\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},\mathsf{n}_{t})
to
I^{i+1}_{a}
. Finally, let
{\mu^{i+1}=\mu^{i}}
, and let

I^{i+1}=I^{i+1}_{a}\cup\Big\{R(t_{1}{}^{I^{i+1}_{a}},\dots,t_{n}{}^{I^{i+1}_{a}})\mid R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\text{ is a fact in }\mu^{i}(\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sigma^{\prime\prime})\Big\}.

These steps are applied exhaustively as long as possible, but the equality step
is applied with higher priority. This process does not terminate in general,
but if it does, the final pair
{\langle I^{n},\mu^{n}\rangle}
in the sequence is
called
the chase
of
\Sigma
and
B
and it contains all query answers:

{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if

{\mathsf{Q}(\mu^{n}(\mathbf{a}))\in I^{n}}
for each fact of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})

where
\mathbf{a}
is a vector of constants and
\mu^{n}(\mathbf{a})
is the vector of
constants obtained by applying
\mu^{n}
to each element of
\mathbf{a}
.

Example 5.

We next discuss how this chase variant is applied to
\Sigma^{\mathit{ex}}
and
B^{\mathit{ex}}
from
Example
4
. As in the standard chase, applying a dependency step to
the TGD (
16
) and facts
S(a_{i},a_{i+1})
introduces facts

R(a_{i},n_{i})
, where
n_{i}
are base labelled nulls since they are introduced by
first-order quantifiers in (
16
). Applying the EGD
(
17
) derives equalities of the form
{n_{i}\approx n_{i+1}}
,
and applying the equality step eventually merges all
n_{i}
into one object
(
n_{1}
in Figure
1
).

Consider now applying dependency steps to the second-order conjuncts
(
18
)–(
20
), all of which contain the function
variable
f
. The chase algorithm needs to produce the same labelled null
whenever
f
is applied to the same argument. The algorithm first applies
(
18
) to
C(a_{1})
. Since this is the first time
f
is applied to

a_{1}
, the algorithm introduces a fresh functional labelled null
n_{2}
and adds
the fact
A(n_{2})
; moreover, the algorithm also introduces an auxiliary fact

\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},n_{2})
(not shown in Figure
1
) to record that
applying
f
to
a_{1}
produces
n_{2}
. Next, the algorithm applies
(
19
) to
C(a_{1})
. The presence of
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},n_{2})
states
that applying
f
to
a_{1}
produces
n_{2}
, and so the dependency step
introduces
U(a_{1},n_{2})
instead of introducing a fresh labelled null. The
algorithm next applies (
20
) to
U(a_{1},n_{2})
; since
f
has
previously not been applied to
n_{2}
, the algorithm introduces a fresh labelled
null
n_{3}
, records this application using an auxiliary fact

\mathsf{V}_{f}(n_{2},n_{3})
, and adds a fact
B(n_{3})
.

Applying the EGD (
21
) to
U(a_{1},n_{2})
derives the equality
{a_{1}\approx n_{2}}
. As in the standard chase, the equality rule handles this by
replacing
n_{2}
with
a_{1}
; hence, facts
A(n_{2})
,
U(a_{1},n_{2})
,

\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},n_{2})
, and
\mathsf{V}_{f}(n_{2},n_{3})
are replaced by
A(a_{1})
,

U(a_{1},a_{1})
,
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},a_{1})
, and
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},n_{3})
, respectively.
However, note that
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},a_{1})
and
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},n_{3})
no longer
provide a unique value for
f
on
a_{1}
. The equality step corrects all such
discrepancies before applying further dependency steps. In our example, this is
achieved by deriving the equality
{a_{1}\approx n_{3}}
, which is handled by
replacing
n_{3}
with
a_{1}
. Thus,
B(n_{3})
and
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},n_{3})
are
replaced by
B(a_{1})
and
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},a_{1})
, respectively (and duplicate
facts are removed).

Finally, consider applying (
15
). The safety condition of
Definition
1
ensures that each variable in a body of
a conjunct occurs in a relational atom, so evaluating the relational body atoms
instantiates all body variables. In our example, variables
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
are
matched to
a_{1}
, and variable
x_{2}
is matched to the labelled null
n_{1}
.
Furthermore, auxiliary fact
\mathsf{V}_{f}(a_{1},a_{1})
reflects that the value of
f

on
a_{1}
is
a_{1}
, so the body equality
{f(x_{1})\approx x_{3}}
is satisfied.
Thus, the dependency step derives
\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})
, as shown in
Figure
1
.

We next briefly discuss the role of the mappings
\mu^{i}
. Since we do not
assume UNA, the chase can replace a constant with a constant, and mappings

\mu^{i}
keep track of such replacements. Thus,
{\mu^{i}(c)=c^{\prime}}
means that
constant
c^{\prime}
represents
c
—that is,
c
and
c^{\prime}
were equated (possibly
indirectly), and so each occurrence of
c
in a fact in
I^{i}
can also be
understood as an occurrence of
c^{\prime}
. These mappings are used in two ways.
First, the dependency step applies
\mu^{i}
to both the body and the head of a
dependency conjunct, which ensures that all constants in the conjunct are
normalised to the constants occurring in
I^{i}
. Second, the final mapping

\mu^{n}
is used to normalise the answer
\mathbf{a}
when checking

{\mathsf{Q}(\mu^{n}(\mathbf{a}))\in I^{n}}
. If UNA is desired, the algorithm can be
modified to report a contradiction when two constants are equated, in which
case mappings
\mu^{i}
need not be maintained.

Fagin et al. (2005b)
presented a chase variant for SO
dependencies without head equality atoms. This chase variant never derives any
equalities, so the facts with
\mathsf{V}_{f}
predicates do not need to be
maintained; instead, the chase derives facts with functional terms of arbitrary
depth. It is possible to extend this chase variant to our generalised SO
dependencies. Roughly speaking, when the equality step is applied to
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
with
{t_{1}\prec t_{2}}
and thus term
t_{2}
is to be replaced by
term
t_{1}
, replacements should be made at proper subterm positions as well;
for example, a fact
A(g(f(t_{2})))
must be normalised to
A(g(f(t_{1})))
. To
ensure completeness, the ordering
\prec
on terms must be
well-founded
,
which intuitively ensures that each term can be normalised in a finite number
of steps. Such an algorithm is equivalent to the chase variant by

Arenas et al. (2011)
that we outlined above, and it can be
seen as an instance of the
paramodulation
calculus used in first-order
theorem proving
(
Nieuwenhuis and Rubio, 2001
)
.

Although the chase does not terminate in general, numerous sufficient
conditions for chase termination on first-order dependencies are known

(
Fagin et al., 2005a
;
Krötzsch and Rudolph, 2011
;
Grau et al., 2013
)
. Moreover, the chase is known to terminate for
all classes of second-order dependencies studied in the literature on schema
mappings
(
Arenas et al., 2011
;
Fagin et al., 2005b
;
Nash et al., 2007
;
Arenas et al., 2013
;
ten Cate et al., 2016
)
, and the results we present in this paper are
applicable in all of these cases.

3.3.
Goal-Driven Query Answering

Computing the chase in full can be expensive, and the computation needs to be
repeated whenever the base instance changes; consequently, chase-based query
answering can be unsuitable for applications where data changes frequently.
However, the amount of work needed to answer a query can be significantly
reduced by using an algorithm that aims to draw only the inferences relevant to
the query. Example
4
illustrates two kinds of opportunity for
optimisation. First,
\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})
remains entailed even if we remove
(
17
) from
\Sigma^{\mathit{ex}}
. In other words, all logical consequences
of a conjunct of a generalised SO dependency can be irrelevant to the query, so
the entire conjunct can be deleted. Second, the derivation of
\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})

depends on the conclusion
R(a_{1},n_{1})
of the conjunct (
16
), but
not on the conclusions
R(a_{i},n_{1})
with
{i\geq 2}
. Thus, we cannot simply
delete conjunct (
16
), but must instead analyse its instances and
identify the relevant ones.

Various
backward chaining
techniques realise goal-driven query answering
by working backwards from the query to identify the premises relevant to the
query answers. For example, SLD resolution
(
Kowalski, 1974
)
in
logic programming searches backwards for proofs from potential query answers.
For relational facts, backward chaining can be roughly realised by matching the
fact in question to the head of a dependency conjunct, and treating the
conjunct’s body as a query whose answers determine all relevant premises—that
is, we ‘invert’ the dependency chase step. However, the premises producing a
fact via the equality chase step are not even present in the step’s result, so
we do not see a practical way to ‘invert’ equality chase steps.

A naïve attempt to overcome this problem might be to treat
\approx
as an
ordinary predicate and axiomatise its properties as discussed in
Section
2
; then, we can use backward chaining for all
inferences, including the ones that involve the equality predicate. However,
such an approach exhibits at least two problems. First, the congruence axioms
(
7
) often produce many redundant inferences: given
{a\approx b}
,
these axioms ‘copy’ all facts containing
a
in some position into facts
containing
b
at that position. This can make the fixpoint computation of

\Sigma
and
\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma)
very inefficient
(
Motik et al., 2015b
)
,
and it can prevent effective backward chaining. Second, as we discussed in
Example
5
, to ensure that function variables are interpreted
as functions, we must introduce a functional reflexivity axiom (
6
)
for each function variable. But then, as soon as we derive
{a\approx b}
, the
functional reflexivity axiom for
f
derives
{f(a)\approx f(b)}
,
{f(f(a))\approx f(f(b))}
, and so on. In other words, even if the chase of a set of
generalised SO dependencies
\Sigma
terminates, the fixpoint computation of

\Sigma
and
\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma)
does not terminate whenever
\Sigma
contains at
least one function variable.

For first-order dependencies, the first problem can be overcome using

singularisation

(
Marnette, 2009
)
—a technique that
omits congruence axioms (
7
) and modifies the dependency bodies so
that joins among body atoms take equalities into account. The technique was
shown to be effective in practice
(
Grau et al., 2013
)
, and

Benedikt et al. (2018)
also used it successfully for goal-driven
query answering over first-order dependencies. This idea was used with
second-order dependencies
(
ten Cate et al., 2016
)
, but the
resulting approach is
incomplete
because it does not take functional
reflexivity into account—that is, on Example
4
, singularisation as
used by
ten Cate et al. (2016)

does not
derive

\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})
. Completeness can be easily recovered by introducing functional
reflexivity axioms, but this prevents termination of fixpoint computation in
the same way as for explicit equality axiomatisation.

To summarise, existing goal-driven query answering techniques are difficult to
apply to dependencies containing equality and function variables. In
Section
4
we present an approach that overcomes these
challenges and is effective in practice.

4.
Query Answering over Generalised Second-Order Dependencies

We now present our goal-driven approach for answering queries over generalised
second-order dependencies. We first discuss in
Section
4.1
the approach end-to-end, and then in
Sections
4.2
–
4.8
we discuss
individual steps in detail.

4.1.
Solution Overview

Algorithm 1

Compute the answers to a query defined by predicate
\mathsf{Q}
over a generalised second-order dependency
\Sigma
and a base instance
B

1:

\Sigma_{1}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma)

2:

\Sigma_{2}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\text{a singularisation of }\Sigma_{1}

3:

P_{3}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{sk}(\Sigma_{2})

4:

P_{4}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{relevance}(P_{3},B)

5:

P_{5}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{magic}(P_{4})

6:

P_{6}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{defun}(P_{5})

7:

P_{7}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{desg}(P_{6})

8:

Compute the chase
\langle I,\mu\rangle
of
P_{7}
and
B
using the chase variant from Section
3.2

9:

Output each fact
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
such that
\mathsf{Q}(\mu(\mathbf{a}))\in I

Our approach is shown in Algorithm
1
. The algorithm takes
as input a generalised SO dependency
\Sigma
and a base instance
B
, and it
outputs all facts of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
such that
{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. This is achieved by transforming
\Sigma
into a
logic program
P_{7}
such that the query answers of
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})}
and

\Sigma
on
B
coincide. All steps apart from the one in
line
4
are independent of
B
: they depend only
on
\Sigma
and the definition of
\mathsf{Q}
within
\Sigma
. Furthermore, while
the transformation in line
4
can be optimised
for a specific base instance, it can always be applied in a way that is
independent of
B
. To analyse equality inferences, several steps of our
transformation treat equality as an ordinary predicate. However, the equality
predicate is treated in the resulting program as ‘true’ equality (modulo
certain details that we discuss shortly), so line
8

can use the chase variant from Section
3.2
that
uses the equality step. In other words, our approach does not impose a
performance penalty in the handling of equality. We next describe the various
stages of our transformation and provide forward pointers to subsections where
each is discussed in depth.

First, the input generalised SO dependency
\Sigma
is transformed to a set of
first-order formulas
\Sigma_{1}
by dropping all second-order quantifiers and
converting function variables into function symbols
(line
1
). We show that this transformation does not
affect query answers. Thus, we can develop our algorithms in the framework of
first-order logic. In particular, many of our proofs use Herbrand models, whose
universe is constructed using function symbols from the signature; however,

\mathbf{f}
in a generalised SO dependency are
quantified second-order
variables
rather than a part of the signature, so the notion of a Herbrand
model does not apply directly. Although we transform the SO dependencies to
first-order formulas, the resulting formulas contain function symbols, so they
are strictly more expressive than standard TGDs and EGDs, and in fact this is
the main source of technical complexity in our work. Although most of our
results are expressed in first-order logic, we begin with second-order
dependencies to position our work within the literature. Second-order
dependencies have attracted significant interest, whereas first-order
dependencies with function symbols, to the best of our knowledge, have not been
studied in this context. As we discuss in
Section
4.2
, the difference between function
variables and function symbols largely disappears in the context of query
answering, but we retain it to keep the formal development precise.

Next, the algorithm applies an extension of the singularisation technique by

Marnette (2009)
that correctly handles second-order
dependencies. This step uses a set of axioms
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)
that relaxes

\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma)
: set
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)
does not contain the congruence axioms
(
7
), and it uses a modified version of the functional reflexivity
axioms (
6
) that does not necessarily imply infinitely many
equalities. The step transforms
\Sigma_{1}
into a singularised set of
dependencies
\Sigma_{2}
(line
2
); this transformation
is not deterministic in the sense that many different
\Sigma_{2}
can be
produced from
\Sigma_{1}
, but any of these can be used in the rest of the
algorithm. We prove that
{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if
and only if
{\Sigma_{2}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma_{2})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
for
each fact
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
—that is, singularisation preserves query answers.
A key issue is to ensure that the modified functional reflexivity axioms derive
all relevant equalities, and this is perhaps the most technically challenging
result of this paper. We discuss singularisation in detail in
Section
4.3
.

The first-order existential quantifiers of
\Sigma_{2}
are next replaced by
function symbols using
Skolemisation
. The transformation is standard and
has been widely used in the literature. However, we apply Skolemisation to

\Sigma_{2}
, where equality is an ordinary predicate. Thus, functional
reflexivity axioms have already been added for the function symbols that
correspond to the function variables in
\Sigma_{1}
, and so no such axioms are
needed for the function symbols introduced by Skolemisation. This observation
opens the door to important optimisations of our approach. Moreover, if

\Sigma
consists only of standard TGDs and EGDs, then no functional
reflexivity axioms are needed at all, and the approach presented in this paper
reduces to the approach for first-order dependencies by

Benedikt et al. (2018)
. The Skolemisation of
\Sigma_{2}
produces a
logic program
P_{3}
(line
3
) that preserves all query
answers:
{\Sigma_{2}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma_{2})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and
only if
{P_{3}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{3})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
for each fact

\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
.

Program
P_{3}
is now amenable to analysing inferences by backward chaining,
which is done in two steps: the relevance analysis
(line
4
) removes from
P_{3}
the rules that
provably do not contribute to query answers, and an adaptation of the magic
sets transformation by
Beeri and Ramakrishnan (1991)

(line
5
) prunes irrelevant inferences from
P_{4}
. We
discuss our techniques in Sections
4.5

and
4.6
, respectively, and show that they preserve query
answers:
{P_{i}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{i})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if

{P_{i+1}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{i+1})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
for each fact

\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
and
{i\in\{3,4\}}
. As we shall see, the two techniques
are complementary: neither technique alone subsumes the other one on our
example.

The original query can now be answered over
{P_{5}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{5})}
, and this
will typically involve fewer inferences than computing the chase of
\Sigma
.
However, equality is still treated as an ordinary predicate in
P_{5}
, so
reasoning with
P_{5}
can be inefficient. The remaining two steps bring
P_{5}

into a form that can be evaluated using the chase variant from
Section
3.2
.

The magic sets transformation can introduce relational body atoms containing
function symbols, which cannot be handled by the chase variant outlined in
Section
3.2
. Thus, function symbols are next
eliminated from all relational body atoms (line
6
).
For example, a rule
{A(f(x))\rightarrow B(f(x))}
is transformed into

{A(z_{f(x)})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x,z_{f(x)})\rightarrow B(f(x))\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x,f(x))}
where
\mathsf{F}_{f}
is a fresh binary predicate uniquely
associated with
f
, and
z_{f(x)}
is a fresh variable uniquely associated
with the term
f(x)
. Intuitively,
\mathsf{F}_{f}
is axiomatised to associate the
terms containing the function symbol
f
with the terms’ arguments, which
allows us to refer to functional terms in the rule body via atoms with the

\mathsf{F}_{f}
predicate. We discuss this step in detail in
Section
4.7
and show that
{P_{5}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{5})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{P_{6}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
for each fact
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
.

The magic sets transformation can introduce rules that are not safe (i.e.,
where a rule variable does not occur in a relational body atom), which also
cannot be handled by the chase variant outlined in
Section
3.2
. Moreover, singularisation can
introduce many equalities in a rule body, which increases the number of joins
needed to evaluate a rule. The final step, which we discuss in
Section
4.8
, reverses singularisation
(line
7
): it eliminates all equalities from rule
bodies, thus making the produced rules safe and easier to evaluate. The
resulting program
P_{7}
preserves all query answers. Note, however, that
program
P_{7}
can contain function symbols that must be handled as outlined in
Examples
4
and
5
. Hence, standard chase variants
for TGDs and EGDs are not sufficient, but query answers can be computed
efficiently as described in Section
3.2
.

The chase computation in line
8
terminates whenever
the fixpoint of
{P_{3}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{3})\cup B}
is finite, which can be ensured in
several ways. First,
Marnette (2009)
showed that, if
\Sigma

is a set of super-weakly acyclic TGDs and EGDs (which is more general than weak
acyclicity by
Fagin et al. (2005a)
), then the fixpoint of
{P_{3}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{3})\cup B}
is finite for each possible singularisation. Second, for
all dependency classes from the schema mapping literature

(
Arenas et al., 2011
;
Fagin et al., 2005b
;
Nash et al., 2007
;
Arenas et al., 2013
;
ten Cate et al., 2016
)
, the fixpoint of
{P_{3}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{3})\cup B}
is
finite regardless of which singularisation is chosen in
line
2
. Third,
Grau et al. (2013)

studied chase termination for singularised dependencies. They showed that the
finiteness of the fixpoint of
{P_{3}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{3})\cup B}
sometimes depends on
the choice of singularisation, and they discussed ways to increase the
likelihood of termination. Our approach is applicable to (at least) all of
these cases.

4.2.
Eliminating Second-Order Quantification

In the first step, we drop all second-order quantifiers in
\Sigma
and convert
the function variables into function symbols. This produces formulas of the
form specified in Definition
1
.

Definition 1.

A
generalised first-order (FO) dependency
has the form
{\forall\mathbf{x}.[\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]}

where
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
and
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
satisfy the same
conditions as in Definition
1
, but all terms use
function symbols instead of function variables.

The notions of the head and body of a generalised FO dependency carry over
naturally from the SO case, and we assume that any query is ‘absorbed’ into a
set of generalised FO dependencies in the same way as in
Definition
3
. The only difference between generalised SO and FO
dependencies is that the former contain function variables, whereas the latter
contain function symbols; we discuss shortly the subtleties of this
distinction. Moreover, note that first-order dependencies such as TGDs and EGDs
do not support function symbols, whereas generalised FO dependencies support
both function symbols and existentially quantified individual variables. Thus,
generalised FO dependencies are strictly more expressive than standard
TGDs and EGDs.

We next show that transforming a generalised SO dependency to a set of
generalised FO dependencies does not affect the query answers.

Definition 2.

For
\Sigma
a generalised SO dependency as in
Definition
1
, set
\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma)
contains the
corresponding generalised FO dependency
{\forall\mathbf{x}_{i}.[\varphi_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}_{i}.\psi_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i})]}
for each
conjunct of
\Sigma
.

Proposition 3.

For each generalised second-order dependency
\Sigma
, each base instance

B
, and each fact of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
, it holds that
{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma)\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

Proof.

We show the contrapositive: there exists an interpretation
I
such that
{I\models_{\approx}B}
,
{I\models_{\approx}\Sigma}
, and
{I\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}

if and only if there exists an interpretation
I^{\prime}
such that
{I^{\prime}\models_{\approx}\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma)\cup B}
and
{I^{\prime}\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. For the
(
\Leftarrow
) direction, we can obtain
I
from
I^{\prime}
by simply ‘erasing’ the
interpretation of the first-order function symbols of
\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma)
. For the
(
\Rightarrow
) direction, we can always extend
I
to
I^{\prime}
by interpreting the
function symbols of
\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma)
in a way whose existence is guaranteed by

{I\models_{\approx}\Sigma}
.
∎

While Proposition
3
may seem to suggest that second-order
quantification is irrelevant to the expressivity of dependencies, the following
example illustrates the differences.

Example 4.

Second-order dependencies were introduced to describe data mappings. For
example, one can use the second-order formula
{\varphi=\exists f.\forall x_{1},x_{2}.[R(x_{1},x_{2})\rightarrow S(x_{1},f(x_{2}))]}
to specify a mapping between
base instances where the source and target instances contain facts with the
R

and
S
predicates, respectively. One possible use of the formula
\varphi
is
to check whether the base instance
{B_{2}=\{S(a,d),S(b,d)\}}
can be
obtained by applying
\varphi
to the base instance
{B_{1}=\{R(a,c),R(b,c)\}}
. To achieve this in our framework, we can form an interpretation
I
as
the union of
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
—that is, we set the interpretation domain to

{\Delta^{I}=\{a,b,c,d\}}
, and we interpret the predicates as
{R^{I}=\{\langle a,c\rangle,\langle b,c\rangle\}}
and
{S^{I}=\{\langle a,d\rangle,\langle b,d\rangle\}}
; then, our task corresponds to checking
whether
I
satisfies
\varphi
. For
\pi
a valuation where
{f^{\pi}(c)=d}
,
we have
{I,\pi\models_{\approx}\forall x_{1},x_{2}.[R(x_{1},x_{2})\rightarrow S(x_{1},f(x_{2}))]}
, so
{I\models_{\approx}\varphi}
. Now let
{\varphi^{\prime}=\forall x_{1},x_{2}.[R(x_{1},x_{2})\rightarrow S(x_{1},f(x_{2}))]}
be the first-order formula
obtained from
\varphi
by the transformation in Definition
2
; note
that
f
in
\varphi^{\prime}
is a function symbol rather than a function variable.
The question whether
I
satisfies
\varphi^{\prime}
is ill-formed since
f^{I}
is not
defined in
I
; thus, formula
\varphi^{\prime}
does not describe a transformation of

B_{1}
into
B_{2}
.

Since base instances cannot contain function symbols, first-order formulas with
function symbols cannot describe transformations between base instances.
Second-order quantification overcomes this problem. In our example, function
variable
f
is not interpreted in
I
, so, to check whether
{I\models_{\approx}\varphi}
holds, we need to guess a valuation for
f
. In contrast, the
interpretation for the function symbol
f
is given explicitly in the
interpretation
I^{\prime}
, so there is nothing to guess. Consequently, checking
satisfaction of second-order dependencies is
NP
-hard in
data complexity
(
Fagin et al., 2005b
)
, whereas the problem is
polynomial for first-order dependencies with function symbols.

Query answering, however, is based on entailment: we identify facts that hold
in
all
models of a base instance and a generalised SO dependency. Since
second-order quantifiers do not occur in the scope of other quantifiers in a
generalised SO dependency, we can eliminate SO quantifiers as in
Definition
2
in a way that can be understood as ‘second-order
Skolemisation’. This transformation does not produce an equivalent formula
(e.g.,
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are not equivalent), but it does not affect
query answers.

This discussion reveals that the chase variant from
Section
3.2
can also be used to reason with
generalised FO dependencies by handling function symbols in exactly the same
way as function variables. The only difference is how to interpret the facts
with the auxiliary
\mathsf{V}_{f}
predicates in the chase result: with generalised
SO dependencies, these describe a valuation for the existentially quantified
function variables
\mathbf{f}
; in contrast, with generalised FO dependencies,
they provide an interpretation for the function symbols in a universal model.
Either way, a query can be answered by computing the chase result and then
reading off the
\mathsf{Q}
facts consisting of only constants.

4.3.
Singularisation of Generalised First-Order Dependencies

As we explained in Section
4.1
, we use the

singularisation
technique by
Marnette (2009)
to
axiomatise the properties of equality in order to be able to analyse equality
inferences. The key idea is that replicating the consequences of equality via
congruence axioms can be avoided if we modify the dependency bodies so that
joins among body atoms take equalities into account. We discuss this intuition
in detail in Example
7
. This idea was used to optimise sufficient
conditions for chase termination and was shown to be very effective in practice

(
Grau et al., 2013
)
. Furthermore,

ten Cate et al. (2016)
applied singularisation to second-order
dependencies, but their formulation does not take into account functional
reflexivity axioms and is thus incomplete: it
does not
derive

\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})
on Example
4
. Completeness can easily be recovered by
simply including functional reflexivity axioms, but this prevents chase
termination as we discuss in Example
7
below.

We next present a variant of singularisation that overcomes both issues. Our
formulation includes functional reflexivity restricted to only the ‘relevant’
domain elements that occur in the interpretation of a relational predicate. In
other words, functional reflexivity is restricted to derive only equalities
that can affect a fact with a relational predicate.

Definition 5.

Let
{\delta=\forall\mathbf{x}.[\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]}
be a generalised first-order dependency. A

singularisation step
is applicable to argument
t_{k}
of an atom

{R(t_{1},\dots,t_{k},\dots,t_{m})}
occurring in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
if
predicate
R
is different from
\approx
and term
t_{k}
is

•

a constant,

•

a variable that occurs as the
i
-th argument of
{R(t_{1},\dots,t_{k},\dots,t_{m})}
where
{i\neq k}
, or

•

a variable that occurs in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
as an argument of a
relational atom other than
{R(t_{1},\dots,t_{k},\dots,t_{m})}
.

Applying such a step to
\delta
replaces
{R(t_{1},\dots,t_{k},\dots,t_{m})}
with
{R(t_{1},\dots,x^{\prime},\dots,t_{m})\wedge x^{\prime}\approx t_{k}}
where

x^{\prime}
is a fresh variable. A generalised FO dependency
\delta^{\prime}
is a

singularisation
of
\delta
if there exists a sequence of
generalised FO dependencies
{\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}}
with

{n\geq 1}
satisfying the following conditions.

•

{\delta_{0}=\delta}
and
{\delta_{n}=\delta^{\prime}}
.

•

If
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
does not contain
\mathsf{Q}
, then

{\delta_{1}=\delta_{0}}
; otherwise,
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is of the
form
{\mathsf{Q}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}
, and
\delta_{1}
is defined as follows
where
{x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{m}^{\prime}}
are fresh variables.

\delta_{1}=\forall\mathbf{x},x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{m}^{\prime}.[\varphi(\mathbf{x})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{m}\approx x_{m}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{m}^{\prime})]

•

For each
{i\in\{2,\dots,n\}}
, dependency
\delta_{i}
is
obtained by applying a singularisation step to
\delta_{i-1}
.

•

No singularisation step is applicable to
\delta_{n}
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set of generalised first-order dependencies. A set

\Sigma^{\prime}
is a
singularisation
of
\Sigma
if each element of

\Sigma^{\prime}
is a singularisation of a distinct dependency of
\Sigma
.
Moreover, set
\mathsf{D}(\Sigma)
contains a domain axiom (
1
)
instantiated for each constant
c
occurring in
\Sigma
, and a domain
axiom (
2
) instantiated for each
n
-ary predicate
R

occurring in
\Sigma
different from
\approx
and
\mathsf{Q}
and each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
. Set
\mathsf{R}
contains the reflexivity axiom
(
3
). Set
\mathsf{ST}
contains the symmetry axiom (
4
) and
the transitivity axiom (
5
). Set
\mathsf{FR}(\Sigma)
contains a

\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity
axiom (
22
)
instantiated for each
n
-ary function symbol
f
occurring in
\Sigma
.
Finally,
{\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)=\mathsf{D}(\Sigma)\cup\mathsf{R}\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(\Sigma)}
.

(22)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{1}^{\prime})\wedge\dots\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{n})\wedge x_{n}\approx x_{n}^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\rightarrow f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\approx f(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})

Note that
\mathsf{D}(\Sigma)
does not contain the domain rules for
\mathsf{Q}
because
this predicate occurs only in the rule head. We define
\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma)
as in
Section
2
while treating
\Sigma
as a conjunction;
again, the domain axioms are not instantiated for
\mathsf{Q}
or
\approx
.

A singularisation of a set of generalised first-order dependencies
\Sigma
is
not unique because the result depends on the order in which singularisation
steps are applied. However, the set
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)
depends only on the function
and predicate symbols in
\Sigma
, which are unaffected by singularisation;
thus,
{\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})=\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)}
for each singularisation
\Sigma^{\prime}
of

\Sigma
. Theorem
6
states that, regardless of how singularisation
steps are applied, query answers remain preserved even though functional
reflexivity axioms are
\mathsf{D}
-restricted. We discuss the intuition behind this
result in Example
7
.

Theorem 6.

For each finite set of generalised first-order dependencies
\Sigma
, each
singularisation
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
, each base instance
B
, and each
fact of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
, it is the case that
{\Sigma\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

Example 7.

For the generalised second-order dependency
\Sigma^{\mathit{ex}}
from
Example
4
, set
{\Sigma^{\prime}=\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma^{\mathit{ex}})}
consists of generalised
first-order dependencies (
15
)–(
21
). As we discuss
shortly, singularisation is applicable only to dependencies (
15
)
and (
17
), which we restate below for convenience.

(
15
restated)

\displaystyle R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge f(x_{1})\approx x_{3}\wedge A(x_{3})\wedge B(x_{3})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{Q}(x_{1})

(23)

\displaystyle R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge f(x_{1})\approx x_{3}\wedge A(x_{3})\wedge x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}\wedge B(x_{3}^{\prime})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime})

(
17
restated)

\displaystyle R(x_{2},x_{1})\wedge S(x_{2},x_{3})\wedge R(x_{3},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow x_{1}\approx x_{4}

(24)

\displaystyle R(x_{2},x_{1})\wedge x_{2}\approx x_{2}^{\prime}\wedge S(x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3})\wedge x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}\wedge R(x_{3}^{\prime},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow x_{1}\approx x_{4}

To singularise dependency (
15
), since the head atom contains the
query predicate, we introduce a fresh variable
x_{1}^{\prime}
, we replace
\mathsf{Q}(x_{1})

with
\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime})
, and we add
{x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}}
to the body. Moreover,
variable
x_{3}
occurs in relational body atoms
A(x_{3})
and
B(x_{3})
, so we
replace
B(x_{3})
with
B(x_{3}^{\prime})
and add
{x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}}
to the body. Thus,
dependency (
23
) is a singularisation of (
15
).
Analogously, dependency (
24
) is a possible singularisation of
dependency (
17
). Singularisation is not applicable to any
other dependency of
\Sigma^{\prime}
, so let
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
be obtained from
\Sigma^{\prime}
by
replacing (
15
) and (
17
) with (
23
) and
(
24
), respectively.

Figure
2
shows a universal model of
{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}
and
B^{\mathit{ex}}
constructed using the chase variant from
Section
3.2
. Equalities are shown as double

\approx
-labelled arcs, where reflexive and transitive edges are omitted for
readability. Moreover,
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
are base labelled nulls, whereas

\mathsf{n}_{s}
,
\mathsf{n}_{t}
, and
\mathsf{n}_{u}
are functional labelled nulls for
the terms
{s=f(a_{1})}
,
{t=f(\mathsf{n}_{s})}
, and
{u=f(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
,
respectively.

We first consider how
\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})
is derived when equality is axiomatised
explicitly as discussed in Section
2
. Then,
\Sigma^{\prime}
and

B
derive
{\mathsf{n}_{s}\approx\mathsf{n}_{t}}
,
{A(\mathsf{n}_{s})}
, and

{B(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
, and the congruence axioms in
\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma^{\prime})
‘copy’ the last
two facts to
{A(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
and
{B(\mathsf{n}_{s})}
. We can thus match the
conjunction
{A(x_{3})\wedge B(x_{3})}
in (
15
) to
{A(\mathsf{n}_{s})}

and
{B(\mathsf{n}_{s})}
, or to
{A(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
and
{B(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
; either
way, we derive
\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})
.

Singularisation avoids fact ‘copying’. Apart from the restricted functional
reflexivity axioms that we discuss shortly, the main difference between

\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})
and
\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma^{\prime})
is that the former does not contain any
congruence axioms. As one can see in Figure
2
, applying the chase
to
{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}
and
B^{\mathit{ex}}
derives
{A(\mathsf{n}_{s})}
and

{B(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
, but not
{A(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
or
{B(\mathsf{n}_{s})}
. To
compensate for this, conjunction
{A(x_{3})\wedge B(x_{3})}
in (
15
)
is replaced with conjunction
{A(x_{3})\wedge x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}\wedge B(x_{3}^{\prime})}

in (
23
). By matching these atoms to
A(\mathsf{n}_{s})
,
{\mathsf{n}_{s}\approx\mathsf{n}_{t}}
, and
{B(\mathsf{n}_{t})}
, respectively, we derive

\mathsf{Q}(a_{1})
without any fact ‘copying’.

Although
x_{1}
occurs in dependency (
23
) in atoms
{f(x_{1})\approx x_{3}}
and
R(x_{1},x_{2})
, the two occurrences of
x_{1}

do not
need to be
renamed apart because singularisation preserves equality facts. In particular,
atom
{f(x_{1})\approx x_{3}}
is true for the same values of
x_{1}
and
x_{3}

after singularisation, so the atom can be matched to the values of
x_{1}
and

x_{3}
that make
R(x_{1},x_{2})
and
A(x_{3})
true.

The functional reflexivity axioms of
\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})
differ
in that axioms (
22
) contain atoms
\mathsf{D}(x_{i})
and
\mathsf{D}(x_{i}^{\prime})
for
each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
. If
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})
were to contain
(
6
) instead of (
22
), then the chase of
{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}
and
B^{\mathit{ex}}
would introduce facts of the form

{\mathsf{n}_{v_{i}}\approx\mathsf{n}_{v_{i+1}}}
for each
i\geq 0
, where
v_{0}=u

and
{v_{i+1}=f(\mathsf{n}_{v_{i}})}
. In other words, a universal model would be
necessarily infinite. Our formulation of
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})
makes the universal
model finite as follows. First, the domain axioms (
1
) and
(
2
) ensure that the chase of
{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}
and

B^{\mathit{ex}}
derives
\mathsf{D}(t)
for each
t
occurring in a relational fact; in other
words, predicate
\mathsf{D}
is axiomatised to enumerate the relevant subset of the
universal model’s domain. Second, the
\mathsf{D}
-atoms in (
22
) prevent
the functional reflexivity axioms from being applied beyond this relevant
subset. The missing equalities that would be produced by the unrestricted
functional reflexivity axioms are irrelevant due to safety. For example,

{\mathsf{n}_{v_{1}}\approx\mathsf{n}_{v_{0}}}
cannot be matched to atom
{x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}}
of dependency (
23
) since variable
x_{3}
also occurs in a
relational atom, and
\mathsf{n}_{v_{1}}
does not occur in any relational fact in a
universal model of
{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}
and
B^{\mathit{ex}}
.

4.4.
Skolemisation and Transformation to a Logic Program

In the rest, we assume that the set of function symbols is partitioned into
disjoint subsets of
true function symbols
and
Skolem function
symbols
. We also assume that the result
\Sigma_{2}
of singularisation contains
only true function symbols. Set
\Sigma_{2}
is transformed into a logic program

{P_{3}=\mathsf{sk}(\Sigma_{2})}
as specified in Definition
8
, which uses
Skolem function symbols.

Definition 8.

The
Skolemisation
of a generalised FO dependency
{\delta=\forall\mathbf{x}.[\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]}

is the set
\mathsf{sk}(\delta)
that contains precisely a rule
{H\leftarrow\varphi(\mathbf{x})}
for each atom
{H\in\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}
, where
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

are the
frontier variables
of
\delta
(i.e., all variables of
\mathbf{x}
that occur in
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
) and
\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
is the
conjunction obtained from
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
by replacing each
existentially quantified variable
{y\in\mathbf{y}}
with
g(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
for

g
a Skolem function symbol freshly introduced for
y
. The

Skolemisation
of a set
\Sigma
of generalised FO dependencies is
the logic program
{\mathsf{sk}(\Sigma)=\bigcup_{\delta\in\Sigma}\mathsf{sk}(\delta)}
.

Note that one can define Skolemisation to use
g(\mathbf{x})
instead of
g(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, which is also semantically correct. However, the formulation from
Definition
8
can be more efficient in practice since it reduces the
arity of the Skolem function symbols.

Let
P
be a logic program that can contain true and Skolem function symbols.
Then,
\mathsf{EQ}(P)
is a set of rules defined analogously to
Section
2
, but where functional reflexivity rules
(
6
) are instantiated only for true function symbols. Moreover,

\mathsf{D}(P)
,
\mathsf{ST}
,
\mathsf{FR}(P)
, and
\mathsf{SG}(P)
are sets of rules as in
Definition
5
, but the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity rules
(
22
) are again instantiated only for true function symbols. We
next show that Skolemisation does not affect query answers.

Proposition 9.

For each set of generalised FO dependencies
\Sigma
, each base instance

B
, each fact of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
, and
{P=\mathsf{sk}(\Sigma)}
, it
holds that
{\Sigma\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and
only if
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

Proof.

Entailment relation
\models
treats equality as an ordinary predicate, so

{\Sigma\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if

{\mathsf{sk}(\Sigma\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma))\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
for each fact of
the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})

(
Fitting, 1996
)
. Furthermore,
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)

does not contain an existential quantifier, so
{\mathsf{sk}(\Sigma\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma))=\mathsf{sk}(\Sigma)\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma)=P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
.
∎

Definition
10
captures the syntactic form of the rules
produced from a generalised SO dependency by removing second-order
quantification, and then singularising and Skolemising the result. The
definition allows relational atoms in rule bodies to contain constants (but not
functional terms), which is more general than the form produced solely by the
transformations presented thus far. However, this allows us to capture the
result of the transformation in Section
4.5
using the
same definition. As we shall see, this syntactic form plays a central role in
our correctness proofs.

Definition 10.

A conjunction of atoms
\phi
is
well-formed
if all relational atoms
of
\phi
are of depth zero, all equality atoms of
\phi
are of depth at
most one, and each variable occurring in
\phi
in an equality atom also
occurs in
\phi
in a relational atom. A rule
r
is
well-formed
if
it is of the form

•

{H\leftarrow\phi}
where conjunction
\phi
is well-formed and
atom
H
is of depth at most one, or

•

{\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{t})\leftarrow\phi\wedge\phi^{\prime}}
where atom

\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{t})
is of depth zero, conjunction
\phi
is well-formed,
and conjunction
\phi^{\prime}
contains zero or more equalities of the form

{x\approx x^{\prime}}
where
x
is a variable that occurs in
\phi
and
x^{\prime}

is a variable that occurs in
\mathbf{t}
but not
\phi
.

A program
P
is
well-formed
if each rule in
P
is
well-formed.

) for

.

Figure 3.
A Herbrand model for the logic program and dataset from Example
11

We next discuss why distinguishing true and Skolem function symbols is
important. This distinction is enabled by the fact that axiomatisation of
equality (either in full or via singularisation) is applied
before

Skolemisation. In particular, at the point when equality is axiomatised, the
formulas in question contain only true function symbols, and so the result
contains only the (
\mathsf{D}
-restricted) functional reflexivity axioms for the true
function symbols. After this step, the equality predicate is ordinary, and
Skolemisation in first-order logic without equality does not require
introducing any functional reflexivity axioms. Thus, after equality
axiomatisation, both
\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma)
and
\mathsf{SG}(P)
contain (
\mathsf{D}
-restricted)
functional reflexivity axioms only for the true function symbols, but not for
the Skolem function symbols. Example
12
clarifies this
important detail.

Example 12.

Let
\Sigma
contain the first-order dependencies (
32
) and
(
33
); note that
\Sigma
is a singularisation of itself as
per Definition
5
. Moreover, let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the Skolemisation of

\Sigma
—that is,
\Sigma^{\prime}
contains dependencies (
32
)
and (
34
). Finally, let
{B=\{R(a,b),A(a),A(b)\}}
.

(32)

\displaystyle R(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow x_{1}\approx x_{2}

(33)

\displaystyle A(x)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\exists y.S(x,y)

(34)

\displaystyle A(x)

\displaystyle\rightarrow S(x,f(x))

Let
I
be the interpretation where
{\Delta^{I}=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}}
,

{a^{I}=b^{I}=\alpha}
,
{A^{I}=\{\alpha\}}
, and
{S^{I}=\{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle,\langle\alpha,\gamma\rangle\}}
. One can see that
{I\models_{\approx}\Sigma}
and
{I\models_{\approx}B}
; in fact,
I
is a universal model of

\Sigma
and
B
. Note that
\exists y.S(x,y)
in dependency
(
33
) says that, for each value of
x
, atom
S(x,y)
must
be satisfied for
at least
one value of
y
, but there is no upper bound
on the number of such values. In fact, when
x
is mapped to the domain element

\alpha
of
I
, two values for
y
satisfy
S(x,y)
:
\beta
and
\gamma
.

Now let
J
be the Herbrand interpretation as specified below. One can check
that
J
is a model of
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
. Constants
a
and

b
are equal in
J
, which is analogous to the condition
{a^{I}=b^{I}=\alpha}
from the previous paragraph. Furthermore,
f
is a Skolem function
symbol, so
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})
does not contain a
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional
reflexivity rule for
f
and thus
J

does not
need to contain
{f(a)\approx f(b)}
. Intuitively,
f(a)
and
f(b)
play in
J
the role of domain
elements
\beta
and
\gamma
in
I
; hence, just as
I
is allowed to contain
distinct
\beta
and
\gamma
, so is
J
allowed to contain distinct
f(a)
and

f(b)
.

\displaystyle J=\{R(a,b),A(a),A(b),S(a,f(a)),S(b,f(b)),a\approx a,a\approx b,b\approx a,b\approx b,f(a)\approx f(a),f(b)\approx f(b)\}

Analogous observations apply when equality is axiomatised fully:
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
is satisfied in a model that extends
J
but does not
need to contain
{f(a)\approx f(b)}
.

Now assume that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is given in the input—that is, the input contains

f
as a true function symbol. We must then interpret
f
as a function from
the very beginning. For example, to define
f^{I}
, we must decide whether

f^{I}(\alpha)
produces
\beta
or
\gamma
. To reflect this when axiomatising
equality, set
\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})
must include the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional
reflexivity axiom for
f
; but then, to obtain a Herbrand model of
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
, we must extend
J
with
{f(a)\approx f(b)}
and

{f(b)\approx f(a)}
.

The observations from Example
12
have repercussions
throughout this paper. First, line
8
of
Algorithm
1
is applied to a program
P_{7}
that contains
true and Skolem function symbols, so the chase variant from
Section
3.2
must be extended to distinguish these
two types. In particular, the algorithm should interpret a ground term
{t=f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})}
with
f
a Skolem function symbol as the functional labelled
null
\mathsf{n}_{t}
unique for
t
, but it should
not
maintain facts of
the form
\mathsf{V}_{f}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v)
for
f
since Skolem function symbols are
not subjected to functional reflexivity axioms.

Second, the need to distinguish true and Skolem function symbols explains why
the correctness of Algorithm
1
is stated in
Theorem
29
the way it is; we discuss this further in
Section
4.8
.

Third, this distinction ensures that, when our approach is applied to
first-order dependencies only, no (
\mathsf{D}
-restricted) functional reflexivity
axioms are necessary. Thus, Algorithm
1
then reduces to
the work by
Benedikt et al. (2018)
, where the chase in
line
8
uses a more standard formulation.

4.5.
Relevance Analysis

The objective of the steps presented thus far was to bring the input into a
form that allows us to identify the inferences that contribute to the query
answers. We now present our first such technique that we call
relevance
analysis
. The technique eliminates entire rules that provably do not
contribute to the query answers. Top-down query answering techniques such as
SLD resolution
(
Kowalski, 1974
)
can naturally identify relevant
inferences by working backwards from the query. However, our objective is to
optimise the Skolemised program rather than answer the query itself, so our
technique must be cheaper to compute than top-down query answering to be of
practical use.

Our technique can be summarised as follows. First, we homomorphically embed the
input base instance
B
into a much smaller instance
B^{\prime}
that we call an

abstraction
of
B
. Second, we compute the fixpoint of the Skolemised
program and
B^{\prime}
; since
B^{\prime}
is orders of magnitude smaller than
B
, the
overhead needed to achieve this should be negligible. Third, inspired by SLD
resolution, we use this fixpoint to analyse inferences of the Skolemised
program. The existence of a homomorphism from
B
to
B^{\prime}
guarantees that each
rule instance that fires on
B
also fires on
B^{\prime}
; hence, a rule that is not
relevant on
B^{\prime}
cannot be relevant on
B
. To present this approach formally,
we next introduce the notion of an abstraction of a base instance.

Definition 13.

A base instance
B^{\prime}
is an
abstraction
of a base instance
B
with
respect to a program
P
if there exists a mapping
\eta
, called a

homomorphism from

B

to

B^{\prime}

w.r.t.

P
, of constants
to constants such that
{\eta(c)=c}
for each constant
c
occurring in

P
, and
{\eta(B)\subseteq B^{\prime}}
where

\eta(B)=\{R(\eta(c_{1}),\dots,\eta(c_{n}))\mid R(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\in B\}.

Any abstraction of
B
can be used in our relevance analysis algorithm, and the
choice of abstraction can affect the algorithm’s precision. However, the

critical instance
for a base instance
B
and program
P
allows us to
abstract
B
in a way that depends on the predicates but not the facts of
B
.
Let
C
be the set of constants of
P
, and let
\ast
be a fresh constant;
then, the critical instance for
B
and
P
is the instance
B^{\prime}
that contains
a fact
{R(\mathbf{a})}
for each
n
-ary predicate
R
occurring in
B
and each

{\mathbf{a}\in(C\cup\{\ast\})^{n}}
. We can further refine this approach if
predicates are
sorted
(i.e., each predicate position is associated with
a sort such as strings or integers): we introduce a distinct fresh constant

\ast_{i}
per sort, and we form
B^{\prime}
as before while honouring the sorting
requirements.

Our relevance analysis technique is shown in Algorithm
2
. It
takes as input a well-formed program
P
and a base instance
B
, and it
returns the rules that are relevant to answering
\mathsf{Q}
on
B
. It selects an
abstraction
B^{\prime}
of
B
with respect to
P

(line
1
), computes the consequences
I
of
P
on

B^{\prime}
(line
2
), and identifies the rules
of
P
that contribute to the answers of
\mathsf{Q}
on
B^{\prime}
by a form of backward
chaining. It populates the ‘ToDo’ set
\mathcal{T}
with facts that represent
the answers to
\mathsf{Q}
on
B
(line
3
) and then
iteratively explores
\mathcal{T}

(lines
5
–
12
). In each
iteration, it extracts a fact
F
from
\mathcal{T}

(line
6
) and identifies each rule
{r\in P\cup\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(P)}
and substitution
\nu
that matches the head of

r
to
F
and the body of
r
to facts in
I

(line
7
). Such
\nu
captures ways of deriving a
fact represented by
F
from
B
via
r
. Thus, if such
\nu
exists, rule
r

is relevant and is added to the set
\mathcal{R}
of relevant rules provided that
{r\not\in\mathsf{SG}(P)}
(line
8
). Rule
r
is not added to

\mathcal{R}
if
{r\in\mathsf{SG}(P)}
: such
r
is part of the axiomatisation of equality,
which can always be recovered from the algorithm’s output. Either way, the
matched body atoms
\mathsf{b}(r)\nu
must all be derivable as well, so they are
added to
\mathcal{T}
(line
11
). The ‘done’ set

\mathcal{D}
ensures that each fact is added to
\mathcal{T}
just once, which
ensures termination.

All rules from
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
apart from the reflexivity rule (
3
)
are considered in line
7
, which is possible
because the rules are well-formed. For example, assume that the body of a rule

r
contains an equality
{x\approx x^{\prime}}
that is matched to a fact
{t\approx t}
. There may be many ways to derive
{t\approx t}
, but most of them are
irrelevant: rule
r
is well-formed, so variable
x
occurs in the body of
r

in a relational atom
A(\dots,x\dots)
that is matched to a fact of the form

A(\dots,t,\dots)
; hence, we can always derive
{t\approx t}
from

A(\dots,t,\dots)
, the domain rule for
A
, and the reflexivity rule. In
contrast, if we considered the reflexivity rule in
line
7
, our algorithm would necessarily identify
each rule that derives a fact of the form
B(\dots,t,\dots)
as relevant and
thus be considerably less effective. Since the reflexivity rule is not
considered, the domain rules (
2
) can be considered in
line
7
only via atoms
\mathsf{D}(x_{i})
and
\mathsf{D}(x_{i}^{\prime})
of
the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity axioms (
22
), which in
turn are considered only if the input program contains true function symbols.
In other words, if the input dependencies are all first-order, neither the
reflexivity rule nor the domain rules are considered.

We can further optimise this algorithm if we know that
{P\cup B}
satisfies
UNA (e.g., if an earlier UNA check was conducted). If
\nu
matches an equality
atom
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
to
{c\approx c}
, then any
corresponding derivation from
P
and
B
necessarily matches
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
to
{d\approx d}
for some constant
d
. Moreover, program
P
is
well-formed, so
{d\approx d}
can be derived using the reflexivity rule, and
no other proofs for
{d\approx d}
need to be considered
(line
10
). Finally, if all matches of
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
are of the form
{c\approx c}
and at least one of
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
is a
variable and the other term is of depth zero, then we can eliminate
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
from the rule and replace the variable everywhere else in the
rule. To this end, Algorithm
2
uses a set
\mathcal{B}
to
keep track of the body equality atoms that can be matched to an equality of the
form other than
{d\approx d}
(line
12
) and are thus
‘blocked’ from being eliminated. After considering all ways to derive query
answers, body equality atoms that have not been blocked are removed
(lines
13
–
16
).

Algorithm 2

\mathsf{relevance}(P,B)

1:

choose
an abstraction
B^{\prime}
of
B
with respect to
P

2:

I\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=T_{P^{\prime}}^{\infty}(B^{\prime})
for
P^{\prime}=P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)

3:

\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})\in I\mid\mathbf{a}\text{ is a tuple of constants}\}

4:

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset

5:

while

\mathcal{T}\neq\emptyset

do

6:

choose and remove
a fact
F
from
\mathcal{T}

7:

for

each

{r\in P\cup\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(P)}
and
each
substitution
\nu
such that
\mathsf{h}(r)\nu=F
and
\mathsf{b}(r)\nu\subseteq I

do

8:

if

r\not\in\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(P)

then

add

r
to
\mathcal{R}

9:

for

each

G_{i}\in\mathsf{b}(r)\nu

do

10:

if

G_{i}
is not of the form
c\approx c
with
c
a constant, or
P\cup B
is not known to satisfy UNA
then

11:

if

G_{i}\not\in\mathcal{D}

then

add

G_{i}
to
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{D}

12:

if

P\cup B
is known to satisfy UNA,
G_{i}
is an equality, and
r\not\in\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(P)

then

add

\langle r,i\rangle
to
\mathcal{B}

13:

if

P\cup B
is known to satisfy UNA
then

14:

for

each
rule
r\in\mathcal{R}
and
each
atom of the form
x\approx t
or
t\approx x
at position
i
of
\mathsf{b}(r)

15:

such that
\langle r,i\rangle\not\in\mathcal{B}
, variable
x
occurs in
\mathsf{b}(r)
in a relational atom, and
\mathsf{dep}(t)=0

do

16:

remove

x\approx t
from
r
and replace
x
with
t
in
r

17:

return

\mathcal{R}

The computation of
I
in line
2
may not
terminate in general, but termination is guaranteed for all dependency classes
that we mentioned in Section
4.1
.
Theorem
14
states that the algorithm is
sound
in the
sense that the returned rules are sufficient to correctly answer the query. In
Section
5
we show empirically that the technique can be very
effective in practice.

Theorem 14.

For each well-formed program
P
defining a query
\mathsf{Q}
and each base
instance
B
, program
{\mathcal{R}=\mathsf{relevance}(P,B)}
is well-formed; moreover,
for each fact of the form
{\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, it holds that
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

If computing
T_{P^{\prime}}^{\infty}(B^{\prime})
in
line
2
is difficult (as is the case in
some of our experiments), we can replace each term
{f(\mathbf{x})}
in
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
with a fresh constant
c_{f}
unique for
f
; note that, for each true
function symbol
f
, terms
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and
f(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})
in the

\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity axioms must also be replaced by
c_{f}
.
This does not affect the algorithm’s correctness since
T_{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}^{\infty}(B)
can still be homomorphically embedded into
T_{P^{\prime}}^{\infty}(B^{\prime})
.
Moreover, the resulting program then does not contain function symbols, so the
computation in line
2
necessarily
terminates.

Example 15.

The chase construction in Example
5
never merges constants, so

\Sigma^{\mathit{ex}}
and
B^{\mathit{ex}}
from Example
4
satisfy UNA and we shall assume
that this is known in advance. Consider applying Algorithm
2

to the rules (
25
)–(
31
) from Example
11
.
We use in line
1
the critical instance
{B^{\prime}=\{C(\ast),S(\ast,\ast)\}}
for
B
and
P
, so the computation in
line
2
produces the instance
I
shown
in Figure
4
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{\;\;\;}c@{\;\;\;}c@{\;\;\;}c@{\;\;\;}c@{\;\;\;}c@{\;\;\;}c@{\;\;\;}c@{}}C(\ast)&S(\ast,\ast)&\mathsf{Q}(\ast)&\mathsf{D}(\ast)&\ast\approx\ast\\
R(\ast,g(\ast))&&&\mathsf{D}(g(\ast))&g(\ast)\approx g(\ast)\\
A(f(\ast))&U(\ast,f(\ast))&&\mathsf{D}(f(\ast))&f(\ast)\approx f(\ast)&f(\ast)\approx\ast\\
&&&&&\ast\approx f(\ast)\\
B(f(f(\ast)))&&&\mathsf{D}(f(f(\ast)))&f(f(\ast))\approx f(f(\ast))&f(f(\ast))\approx f(\ast)&f(f(\ast))\approx\ast\\
&&&&&f(\ast)\approx f(f(\ast))&\ast\approx f(f(\ast))\\
&&&&f(f(f(\ast)))\approx f(f(f(\ast)))&f(f(f(\ast)))\approx f(f(\ast))&f(f(f(\ast)))\approx f(\ast)&f(f(f(\ast)))\approx\ast\\
&&&&&f(f(\ast))\approx f(f(f(\ast)))&f(\ast)\approx f(f(f(\ast)))&\ast\approx f(f(f(\ast)))\\
\end{array}
