4.6.
Magic Sets Transformation

We now present a variant of the well-known magic sets transformation that can
eliminate certain irrelevant rule instances. The transformation modifies the
rules of a program and introduces so-called
magic
predicates so that
fixpoint computation of the resulting program simulates backward chaining on
the original program. This transformation does not subsume the relevance
analysis: irrelevant rules in the input give rise to rules that populate
irrelevant magic predicates, which introduces overheads that can be avoided by
first eliminating irrelevant rules. We show empirically in
Section
5
that the two techniques complement each other.

Equality is treated as an ordinary predicate, so we could use the standard
magic sets transformation by
Beeri and Ramakrishnan (1991)
. However, we
improve the algorithm’s effectiveness in two ways: we reduce the number of
magic rules by taking the symmetry of equality into account, and we show that
the reflexivity rule (
3
) does not need to be taken into account in
the transformation. Before discussing our approach, we illustrate the idea
behind the standard magic sets algorithm.

Example 16.

Rule (
53
) shown below derives a fact
B(a)
for each constant
a

that is reachable from some fact
B(b)
via facts with the
R
predicate; thus,
computing the full fixpoint of rule (
53
) could be costly. However,
rule (
52
) derives
\mathsf{Q}(a)
only if both
A(a)
and
B(a)
are
derived. Thus, if we are interested only in facts of the form
\mathsf{Q}(a)
, and
if only a small fraction of the facts derivable by rule (
53
) are
relevant to the query, magic sets can avoid computing the full fixpoint of the
rule.

(52)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}(x)

\displaystyle\leftarrow A(x)\wedge B(x)

(53)

\displaystyle B(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge B(x_{2})

The idea is to simulate sideways information passing from techniques such as
SLD resolution. To evaluate the body of rule (
52
), we shall
identify all values of
x_{1}
that make
A(x_{1})
true, and then pass those
values to atom
B(x_{1})
. Furthermore, we shall use these bindings for
B(x_{1})

to constrain the evaluation of rule (
53
): for each binding
x_{1}
,
we shall identify all values
x_{2}
that make the atom
R(x_{1},x_{2})
true, and we
shall pass those values of
x_{2}
to the atom
B(x_{2})
. To determine the order
in which to evaluate the body atoms and how to propagate bindings, the magic
sets algorithm uses a
sideways information passing strategy
(SIPS),
which
adorns
each predicate in a rule with a string consisting of
letters
\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{f}
. If the
i
-th letter is
\mathsf{b}
, this indicates
that the
i
-th argument is
bound
—that is, a value for the argument is
passed from the rule head or an earlier atom in the rule body; otherwise, the
argument is
free
. Only predicates occurring in a rule head are adorned;
in our example, this includes predicates
\mathsf{Q}
and
B
. When applied to our
example, the SIPS can produce the following rules.

(54)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}^{\mathsf{f}}(x)

\displaystyle\leftarrow A(x)\wedge B^{\mathsf{b}}(x)

(55)

\displaystyle B^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge B^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{2})

In rule (
54
), the adornment of atom
\mathsf{Q}^{\mathsf{f}}(x)
indicates
that all values of
x
are important, whereas the adornment of atom

B^{\mathsf{b}}(x)
indicates that the value for
x
is determined by an earlier atom

A(x)
.

To simulate sideways information passing, the magic sets algorithm introduces a

magic
predicate
\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}
for each predicate
R
adorned with a
string
\alpha
. Such predicates accumulate the relevant bindings using
so-called
magic rules
. In our example,
\mathsf{Q}
is annotated with

\mathsf{f}
in rule (
54
), so we introduce the magic rule
(
56
) to indicate that all bindings of
\mathsf{Q}
should be
produced. Furthermore, rule (
57
) computes the bindings that are
passed from atom
A(x)
to atom
B(x)
in rule (
52
); these bindings
should also be propagated from the head of rule (
53
) to the atom

B(x_{2})
, which is achieved by rule (
58
).

(56)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\mathsf{f}}

\displaystyle\leftarrow

(57)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{B}^{\mathsf{b}}(x)

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\mathsf{f}}\wedge A(x)

(58)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{B}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{2})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{B}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{1})\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2})

Finally, to answer the query, the magic sets transformation introduces the
following
modified rules
, which constrain the original rules
(
52
) and (
53
) using magic predicates.

(59)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}(x)

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\mathsf{f}}\wedge A(x)\wedge B(x)

(60)

\displaystyle B(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{B}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{1})\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge B(x_{2})

One can compute the fixpoint of the transformed program as described in
Section
2
, but the magic rules
(
56
)–(
58
) derive a fact of the form

\mathsf{m}_{B}^{\mathsf{b}}(a)
only if constant
a
is relevant to the query answer.
This, in turn, constrains the evaluation of the modified rules
(
59
) and (
60
) only to the relevant bindings,
which ensures that the computation simulates SLD resolution.

We next formalise a variant of the magic sets transformation that deals with
equality in an optimised fashion. We start by introducing the notions of
adornment and magic predicates.

Definition 17.

An
adornment
for an
n
-ary relational predicate
R
is a string of
length
n
over the alphabet
\mathsf{b}
(‘bound’) and
\mathsf{f}
(‘free’). For
each adornment
\alpha
for
R
with
k

\mathsf{b}
-symbols,

\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}
is a fresh
magic
predicate of arity
k
unique for

R
and
\alpha
. An
adornment
for the equality predicate
\approx

is a string
\mathsf{bf}
or
\mathsf{fb}
, and
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}
is a fresh

magic
predicate for
\approx
of arity one.

Given an adornment
\alpha
of length
n
and an
n
-tuple
{\mathbf{t}}
of
terms,
{\mathbf{t}^{\alpha}}
is a tuple of terms that contains, in the same
relative order, each
{t_{i}\in\mathbf{t}}
for which the
i
-th element of

\alpha
is
\mathsf{b}
.

Relational predicates are thus adorned in the same way as in the standard magic
sets transformation, whereas the equality predicate can be adorned only by

\mathsf{bf}
or
\mathsf{fb}
. In particular, adorning
\approx
by
\mathsf{ff}
would
indicate that the entire equality relation should be computed, and this is
generally very inefficient since the equality relation typically affects most
predicates of a program. Furthermore, adorning the head of the transitivity
rule (
5
) by
\mathsf{bb}
produces the rule

(61)

\displaystyle x_{1}\approx^{\mathsf{bb}}x_{3}\leftarrow x_{1}\approx^{\mathsf{bf}}x_{2}\wedge x_{2}\approx^{\mathsf{bb}}x_{3},

which introduces the adornment
\mathsf{bf}
. Now, adornment
\mathsf{bf}
is more
general than
\mathsf{bb}
: whenever a pair of constants
\langle c_{1},c_{2}\rangle

is a relevant binding for
\approx^{\mathsf{bb}}
, constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are
relevant bindings for
\approx^{\mathsf{bf}}
and
\approx^{\mathsf{fb}}
. Adornment

\mathsf{bb}
is thus subsumed by
\mathsf{bf}
.

Since the predicate
\approx
is symmetric, whenever a constant
c
is a
relevant binding for the first argument of
\approx
, constant
c
is also a
relevant binding for the second argument; thus, introducing distinct magic
predicates
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{\mathsf{bf}}
and
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{\mathsf{fb}}
is redundant.
Consequently, Definition
17
introduces just one magic
predicate
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}
for
\approx
. Notation
{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}
suggests
that one argument is bound and the other one is free, but without fixing the
order of the arguments.

We next adapt the notion of a sideways information passing strategy to our
setting. As in the equality-free case, a SIPS describes how bindings are passed
sideways through rule bodies.

Definition 18.

A
sideways information passing strategy
is a function that takes a
well-formed conjunction
C
of
n
atoms and a set
V
of variables.
Applying the function to
C
and
V
produces

\mathsf{SIPS}(C,V)=\langle\langle R_{1}(\mathbf{t}_{1}),\dots,R_{n}(\mathbf{t}_{n})\rangle,\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle\rangle,

where
{\langle R_{1}(\mathbf{t}_{1}),\dots,R_{n}(\mathbf{t}_{n})\rangle}
is a
permutation of the atoms of
C
, and
{\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle}
is a sequence where each
\gamma_{i}
is an adornment for
R_{i}
.
For each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
, property (
62
) must hold.

(62)

\displaystyle\mathsf{vars}(\mathbf{t}_{i}^{\gamma_{i}})\subseteq V\cup\bigcup\Big\{\mathsf{vars}(\mathbf{t}_{k})\mid 1\leq k<i\Big\}

Furthermore, for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
such that
R_{i}(\mathbf{t}_{i})

is of the form
{t_{i}^{1}\approx t_{i}^{2}}
, and for each
{j\in\{1,2\}}

such that the
j
-th element of
\gamma_{i}
is
\mathsf{b}
, property
(
63
) must hold.

(63)

\displaystyle\mathsf{vars}(t_{i}^{j})\subseteq\bigcup\Big\{\mathsf{vars}(\mathbf{t}_{k})\mid 1\leq k<i\text{ and predicate }R_{k}\text{ is relational}\Big\}

A SIPS is given a conjunction
C
corresponding to a rule body, and a set of
variables
V
that will be bound when the body is evaluated (i.e., variables

V
will be passed as bindings from the head). The SIPS reorders the conjuncts
of
C
and adorns each atom’s predicate to indicate which arguments of the atom
are bound. The result of a SIPS must satisfy two conditions. The condition in
equation (
62
) captures the essence of sideways information
passing: each bound variable that is passed sideways to an atom
R_{i}(\mathbf{t}_{i})

must occur in either
V
or a preceding (relational or equality) atom
R_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k})
. The condition in equation (
63
) requires that each variable
bound in an equality atom also occurs in a preceding relational atom. This is
needed to ensure termination of the fixpoint computation of the transformed
program, but the details are quite technical and rely on the exact formulation
of our approach; thus, we defer a detailed discussion to
Example
24
. For now, we just observe that this
requirement can always be satisfied when conjunction
C
is well formed: each
variable occurring in an equality atom in
C
also occurs in a relational atom,
so we can always reorder all relevant relational atoms before the respective
equality atoms.

With these definitions in place, we present our variant of the magic sets
transformation in Algorithm
3
. The algorithm takes as input a
well-formed program
P
, and it outputs a transformed program
\mathcal{R}
. In
line
1
, the algorithm initialises the ‘ToDo’ set

\mathcal{T}
with the magic predicate
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}
for
\alpha

where all positions are free, and it initialises
\mathcal{R}
to contain the rule

\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}\leftarrow
with the empty body; thus, the resulting
program will entail all facts of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
entailed by
P
. The
algorithm then enters a loop
(lines
2
–
10
) where it successively
processes a magic predicate from
\mathcal{T}
. The set
\mathcal{D}
ensures that
each magic predicate is processed only once. In each iteration, the algorithm
selects a magic predicate
\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}
to process
(line
3
), and it processes each rule
{r\in P\cup\mathsf{D}(P)}
containing
R
in the head
(lines
4
–
10
). If
R
is the equality
predicate, then
\alpha
is
{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}
so the rule
r
is processed by adorning

\mathsf{h}(r)
with both
\mathsf{bf}
and
\mathsf{fb}

(lines
6
–
7
); otherwise,
r

is processed by adorning the head of
r
by
\alpha

(line
10
). Either way, the algorithm extends the result
set
\mathcal{R}
with the modified rule for
r
(line
20
),
consults the SIPS to determine how to propagate the bindings among the atoms of

r
(line
21
), generates the magic rule for each body atom of

r
that also occurs in
P
in a rule head (line
24
),
and adds all freshly generated magic predicates to
\mathcal{T}
for further
processing (line
25
). In line
23
, the
algorithm takes into account that
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}
is the magic
predicate for
\approx
adorned by
\mathsf{bf}
or
\mathsf{fb}
.

If we wanted to use the standard magic sets transformation, we should apply it
to all the rules of
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
. Now assume that
P
contains a rule
{r=C(x)\leftarrow A(x)\wedge x\approx x^{\prime}\wedge B(x^{\prime})}
, and that
r
is
matched to an instance using a substitution
\sigma
where
{\sigma(x)=\sigma(x^{\prime})=t}
—that is, the equality atom
{x\approx x^{\prime}}
is matched to a
fact of the form
{t\approx t}
. This fact can be produced by the reflexivity
rule (
3
) and the domain rule (
2
) for an arbitrary
predicate
R
occurring in
P
. Therefore, when applied to
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
,
the standard magic sets algorithm would process
r
, the reflexivity rule
(
3
), and the domain rules (
2
) for each predicate
R
.
Consequently, the resulting program would necessarily ‘touch’ all predicates
from the input program and would thus likely be large.

Line
4
of Algorithm
3
addresses the issue
from the previous paragraph by excluding the reflexivity rule from processing
and thus considerably reducing the size of the result. This is possible because
the rules of
P
are well formed. In the rule
r
from the previous paragraph,
variables
x
and
x^{\prime}
of equality
{x\approx x^{\prime}}
occur in relational atoms

A(x)
and
B(x^{\prime})
. Thus, if
{x\approx x^{\prime}}
is matched to
{t\approx t}
,
atoms
A(x)
and
B(x^{\prime})
are matched to
A(t)
and
B(t)
; but then, we can
recover
{t\approx t}
using the domain rules for
A
and
B
only, without
considering any other predicate in the input program.

In fact, the symmetry, the transitivity, and the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional
reflexivity rules are not processed in line
4
either: the
resulting program
\mathcal{R}
will be considered together with
\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})
, so there is
no point in adding the modified rules to
\mathcal{R}
. The algorithm only needs the
magic rules obtained from the rules of
\mathsf{SG}(P)
: line
12

introduces the magic rule obtained by annotating the head of the transitivity
rule (
5
) with
\mathsf{bf}
or
\mathsf{fb}
, and
lines
14
–
16
introduce the magic
rules obtained by annotating the head of the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional
reflexivity rules (
22
).

Finally, the magic rules produced in lines
15

and
16
contain the domain predicate
\mathsf{D}
in the body;
hence, if at least one such rule is produced, the domain rules need to be
considered in line
4
to produce the magic rules that
identify the relevant bindings for the
\mathsf{D}
predicate. To ensure that the
domain rules are indeed analysed, the magic predicate
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}
is
added to
\mathcal{T}
in line
8
whenever the
\approx

predicate is processed and the input program
P
contains at least one true
function symbol. In other words, the domain rules are not considered if
P
is
obtained from first-order dependencies.

Algorithm 3

\mathsf{magic}(P)

1:

\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}\}
and
\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}\leftarrow\}
, where
\alpha=\mathsf{f}\cdots\mathsf{f}

2:

while

\mathcal{T}\neq\emptyset

do

3:

choose and remove
some
\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}
from
\mathcal{T}

4:

for

each

{r\in P\cup\mathsf{D}(P)}
such that
\mathsf{h}(r)=R(\mathbf{t})

do

5:

if

R={\approx}

then

\triangleright
Note that
\alpha={\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}

6:

\mathsf{process}(r,\alpha,\mathsf{bf})

7:

\mathsf{process}(r,\alpha,\mathsf{fb})

8:

if

\mathsf{FR}(P)\neq\emptyset
and
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}\not\in\mathcal{D}

then

add

\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}
to
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{D}

9:

else

10:

\mathsf{process}(r,\alpha,\alpha)

11:

if

\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}\in\mathcal{D}

then

12:

add

\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{2}
to
\mathcal{R}

13:

for

each
true function symbol
f
in
P
of arity
n
and
each

i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

do

14:

add

\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))
to
\mathcal{R}

15:

add

\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{i})
to
\mathcal{R}

16:

add

\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{i}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{i})\wedge x_{i}\approx x_{i}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{R}

17:

return

\mathcal{R}

18:

19:

procedure

\mathsf{process}
(
r,\alpha,\beta
) where
\mathsf{h}(r)=R(\mathbf{t})

20:

if

r\not\in\mathsf{D}(P)

then

add

\mathsf{h}(r)\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{t}^{\beta})\wedge\mathsf{b}(r)
to
\mathcal{R}

21:

\langle\langle R_{1}(\mathbf{t}_{1}),\dots,R_{n}(\mathbf{t}_{n})\rangle,\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\rangle\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{SIPS}(\mathsf{b}(r),\mathsf{vars}(\mathbf{t}^{\beta}))

for

each

1\leq i\leq n
such that

or

if

R_{i}={\approx}
and
\gamma_{i}\in\{\mathsf{bf},\mathsf{fb}\}

\mathcal{D}

Note:
Equality atoms of the form
t_{1}\approx t_{2}
are
abbreviations for
{\approx}(t_{1},t_{2})
, so
R(\mathbf{t})
and
R_{i}(\mathbf{t}_{i})

can be equality atoms.

As we already mentioned, programs
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
and
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}

entail the same query answers, but there is an important detail: according to
Definition
5
, program
\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})
would need to contain the domain
rules (
2
) even for the magic predicates. This would not be a
problem from a correctness point of view; however, as we discuss in
Example
23
, this can adversely affect the termination
of the fixpoint computation for
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
. Therefore, in
Definition
19
we refine the definition of
\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})
and
stipulate that the domain rules (
2
) are never instantiated for
magic predicates. Theorem
20
shows that the query answers
nevertheless remain preserved.

Definition 19.

For
P
a well-formed program and
{\mathcal{R}=\mathsf{magic}(P)}
, program
\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})
is
defined as in Definition
5
but without instantiating the domain
rules of the form (
2
) for the magic predicates.

Theorem 20.

For each well-formed program
P
defining a query
\mathsf{Q}
, each base
instance
B
, each fact of the form
{\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, and
{\mathcal{R}=\mathsf{magic}(P)}
, it is the case that
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

Theorem
20
holds regardless of whether the fixpoint of
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B}
is finite. If, however, it is, then
Theorem
21
guarantees that the fixpoint of
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B}
is finite too, so the fixpoint computation terminates. This is
not a trivial observation: the rules of
P
can contain function symbols in the
body, which are transformed into function symbols in the head of the magic
rules. Thus,
\mathcal{R}
can contain rules with function symbols in the head at
positions not originally found in
P
, so it is not obvious that the fixpoint
of
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B}
is necessarily finite.

Theorem 21.

For each well-formed program
P
, each base instance
B
, and programs
{\mathcal{R}=\mathsf{magic}(P)}
,
{P_{1}=P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
, and
{P_{2}=\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
, if

{T_{P_{1}}^{\infty}(B)}
is finite, then
{T_{P_{2}}^{\infty}(B)}
is finite as
well.

22
illustrates how Algorithm
3
is applied to
the rules produced by our relevance analysis algorithm in
Example
15
.

Example 23.

Let
f^{i}

abbreviates

. To understand why the domain rules (

)
should

19
), note that the rules of
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
from

22
derive
\mathsf{D}(f(a_{1}))
,
\mathsf{D}(f^{2}(a_{1}))
, and

\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(f^{2}(a_{1}))
; thus, the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted reflexivity rule
(
22
) for
f
derives
{f^{2}(a_{1})\approx f^{3}(a_{1})}
, and so the
magic rule (
86
) derives
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(f^{3}(a))
. Now, if

\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})
contained rule (

), we would derive
\mathsf{D}(f^{3}(a))
, so
rule (
22
) for
f
would derive
{f^{3}(a_{1})\approx f^{4}(a_{1})}
; but
then, rule (
86
) would derive
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(f^{4}(a))
, rule
(

) would derive
\mathsf{D}(f^{4}(a))
, and the process would never
terminate.

(90)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(x)\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x)

Definition
19
is thus critical to
Theorem
21
. Intuitively, by not instantiating the
domain rules (
2
) for the magic predicates, we ensure that each
fact with the
\mathsf{D}
predicate that is derived by
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
is also
derived by
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
. Hence, the application of the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted
functional reflexivity axioms is constrained in the same way as discussed in
Section
4.3
.

To understand the condition (
63
) from Definition
18
,

) in
line

(so
{\beta=\mathsf{bf}}
) where the SIPS keeps

and
\mathsf{b}

then produces rule

), which makes the fixpoint of the transformed

) of

requires that, if a term in a body equality is

relational atom. Thus, on our example, the SIPS can order the body atoms as in

24

). Although this

92

solves the

program with a predicate that is not a magic predicate are also derived by the
input program. Hence, if the depth of the facts
A(t)
derived by the input

, then the depth of such facts derived by the
transformed program is bounded by

as well; but then, the depth of the facts
derived by rule (
94
) is bounded by
{d+1}
.

4.7.
Removal of Constants and Function Symbols in Rule Bodies

The programs produced by the relevance analysis and/or the magic sets
transformation can be used to answer a query, but treating
\approx
as an
ordinary predicate can make this process inefficient. As we explained in
Section
4.1
, the main objective of our work is to
optimise the input dependencies so that they can be evaluated with ‘true’
equality. Towards this goal, we next present two final transformations that
bring the resulting program into a required form.

The magic sets transformation can introduce rules such as (
85
)
and (
89
) containing relational atoms with function symbols in the
body. The chase variant from Section
3.2
cannot
process such rules, so we next show how to remove the constants and function
symbols from the rule bodies. Intuitively, for each
n
-ary function symbol

f
, we introduce a fresh
n+1
-ary predicate
\mathsf{F}_{f}
, and we introduce
rules that, for each relevant ground term
f(\mathbf{t})
, ensure that
\mathsf{F}_{f}

associates
\mathbf{t}
with
f(\mathbf{t})
. This allows us to rewrite references to
the functional terms in the rule bodies as references to atoms with the

\mathsf{F}_{f}
predicate. This is formalised in Definition
25
.

Definition 25.

Program
\mathsf{defun}(P)
is obtained from a program
P
by exhaustively applying
each of the following steps in the following sequence.

(1)

In the body of each rule, replace each occurrence of a constant

c
with a fresh variable
z_{c}
unique for
c
, add the atom

{\mathsf{F}_{c}(z_{c})}
to the body, and add the rule
{\mathsf{F}_{c}(c)\leftarrow}
.

(2)

In the body of each rule, replace each occurrence of a term of
the form
{f(\mathbf{s})}
with a fresh variable
z_{f(\mathbf{s})}
unique for

f(\mathbf{s})
, and add the atom
{\mathsf{F}_{f}(\mathbf{s},z_{f(\mathbf{s})})}
to
the rule body.

(3)

For each function symbol
f
considered in the previous step, and
for each rule
r
that contains a term of the form
f(\mathbf{t})
in its
head, add the rule
{\mathsf{F}_{f}(\mathbf{t},f(\mathbf{t}))\leftarrow\mathsf{b}(r)}
.

(4)

For each true function symbol
f
occurring in
P
, add the rule

(95)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\leftarrow\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{1}^{\prime})\wedge\dots\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{n})\wedge x_{n}\approx x_{n}^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{n}^{\prime}).

The first step of Definition
25
eliminates all constants, and the
second step eliminates all functional terms from all relational atoms in rule
bodies. The third and the fourth step axiomatise
\mathsf{F}_{f}
: the third step
accounts for the rules of
P
, and the fourth step accounts for the

\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity rules. It is straightforward to see that
all query answers remain preserved.

Proposition 26.

For each program
P
, each base instance
B
, each fact
R(\mathbf{t})
with

R
not of the form
\mathsf{F}_{f}
, and
{\mathcal{R}=\mathsf{defun}(P)}
, it is the case
that
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models R(\mathbf{t})}
if and only if
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B\models R(\mathbf{t})}
.

Proof.

Let
{I=T_{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}^{\infty}(B)}
and let
{J=T_{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}^{\infty}(B)}
. By routine inductions on the construction of
I
and
J
, one
can show that

J=I\cup\{\mathsf{F}_{f}({\mathbf{t},f(\mathbf{t})})\mid f(\mathbf{t})\text{ occurs in }I\text{ and }\mathsf{F}_{f}\text{ occurs in }\mathcal{R}\}.

Each derivation of a fact
F
in
I
by a rule
{r\in P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}

corresponds to the derivation of
F
and
{\mathsf{F}_{f}(\mathbf{t},f(\mathbf{t}))}
for
each term
{f(\mathbf{t})}
occurring in
F
in
J
by the rules obtained from
r

by transformations in Definition
25
; moreover, each term occurring
in a fact with predicate of the form
\mathsf{F}_{f}
also occurs in a fact with a
predicate not of the form
\mathsf{F}_{f}
so the rules in
{\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{R})\setminus\mathsf{D}(P)}
do not derive any new facts. Thus,
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B}
and
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B}
entail the same facts over the predicates of
P
.
∎

Example 27.

Consider again rules (
64
)–(
89
) produced by
applying the magic sets transformation to our running example. Rule
(
65
) contains a functional term in a body equality atom, and it can
be handled directly by the chase for generalised SO dependencies from
Section
3.2
. In contrast, rule (
70
)
also contains a functional term only in the equality atom
{f(x_{1})\approx x_{3}}
, but the variable
x_{1}
does not occur in a relational body atom; thus,
it is unclear how to match the body variables of this rule as described in
Section
3.2
. Furthermore, rules
(
73
), (
85
), and
(
87
)–(
89
) all contain functional terms in
relational atoms in the body. However, the transformation described in
Definition
25
brings these rules into a suitable form as shown
below.

(96)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\mathsf{f}}\wedge B(x_{3}^{\prime})\wedge x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}\wedge A(x_{3})\wedge z_{f(x_{1})}\approx x_{3}\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

(97)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\mathsf{bf}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\mathsf{f}}\wedge B(x_{3}^{\prime})\wedge x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}\wedge A(x_{3})\wedge z_{f(x_{1})}\approx x_{3}\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

(98)

\displaystyle A(f(x))

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{A}^{\mathsf{b}}(z_{f(x)})\wedge C(x)\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x,z_{f(x)})

(99)

\displaystyle R(x_{1},g(x_{1}))

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\mathsf{fb}}(z_{g(x_{1})})\wedge S(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{g}(x_{1},z_{g(x_{1})})

(100)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(z_{f(x_{1})})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

(101)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(z_{f(x_{1})})\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

(102)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(z_{f(x_{1})})\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

Furthermore, to axiomatise predicates
\mathsf{F}_{f}
and
\mathsf{F}_{g}
, the
transformation introduces rules
(
103
)–(
107
) for rules
(
71
), (
98
), (
74
),
(
75
), and (
99
) that contain a function symbol
in the rule head.

(103)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{2},f(x_{2}))

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{B}^{\mathsf{f}}\wedge U(x_{1},x_{2})

(104)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{f}(x,f(x))

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{A}^{\mathsf{b}}(z_{f(x)})\wedge C(x)\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x,z_{f(x)})

(105)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{g}(x_{1},g(x_{1}))

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\mathsf{bf}}(x_{1})\wedge S(x_{1},x_{2})

(106)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{f}(x,f(x))

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{U}^{\mathsf{ff}}\wedge C(x)

(107)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{g}(x_{1},g(x_{1}))

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\mathsf{fb}}(z_{g(x_{1})})\wedge S(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{g}(x_{1},z_{g(x_{1})})

Finally,
f
is a true function symbol, so the transformation also introduces
the following rule. In contrast,
g
is a Skolem function symbol, so no such
rule is needed.

(108)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},f(x_{1}))\leftarrow\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{1}^{\prime})

4.8.
Reversing the Effects of Singularisation

All equalities in the body of a rule obtained by the transformation in
Definition
25
contain only variables, which enables the
transformation presented in the following definition.

Definition 28.

The
desingularisation
of a rule is obtained by removing a body atom
of the form
{x\approx t}
where
x
is a variable, replacing
x
with
t

everywhere else in the rule, and repeating this process as long as
possible. For
P
a program,
\mathsf{desg}(P)
contains a desingularisation of
each rule of
P
.

Desingularisation is useful because it removes equality atoms from the rule
bodies and thus reduces the number of joins. Theorem
29
establishes
correctness of our entire pipeline.

Theorem 29.

Consider an arbitrary generalised second-order dependency
\Sigma
, a base
instance
B
, and the program
P_{7}
obtained by applying
Algorithm
1
to
\Sigma
and
B
. Program
P_{7}
is safe
and contains no equalities, function symbols, or constants in the body.
Moreover, for each fact of the form
{\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, it is the case that

{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

One may wonder whether the condition in Theorem
29
could have been
stated more simply as
{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and
only if
{P_{7}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. The latter property indeed
holds, but it is stronger than what is needed: the entailment relation

\models_{\approx}
uses the standard definitions of first-order logic that do not
distinguish true and Skolem function symbols. By using
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, we do not wish to suggest that one should compute a
fixpoint of
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})}
on
B
to answer a query. Rather, we stress
that the query answers can be computed using the chase variant outlined in
Section
3.2
; this algorithm treats the
\approx

predicate as ‘true’ equality, but it does not enforce functional reflexivity
for the Skolem function symbols.

Example 30.

On our running example, applying desingularisation to rules
(
97
) and (
102
), which we both restate for
convenience, produces rules (
109
) and (
110
),
respectively.

(
97
restated)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\mathsf{bf}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\mathsf{f}}\wedge B(x_{3}^{\prime})\wedge x_{3}\approx x_{3}^{\prime}\wedge A(x_{3})\wedge z_{f(x_{1})}\approx x_{3}\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

(109)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\mathsf{bf}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\mathsf{f}}\wedge B(x_{3})\wedge A(x_{3})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},x_{3})

(
102
restated)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(z_{f(x_{1})})\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

(110)

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{b}}(x_{1})

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(z_{f(x_{1})})\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},z_{f(x_{1})})

Note that the variable
x_{1}^{\prime}
occurs in the body of rule (
102
)
only in the equality atom
{x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}}
, but not in a relational atom;
hence, such a rule cannot be evaluated using the chase variant described in
Section
3.2
. In contrast, rule (
110
)
produced by desingularisation does not contain equality atoms in the body and
can therefore be processed without any problems. All remaining rules are
transformed analogously and we do not show the result for the sake of brevity.

5.
Evaluation

To see whether our techniques can answer a query more efficiently than by
computing the chase in full, we have implemented a prototype and have evaluated
it on a subset of the first-order benchmarks by

Benedikt et al. (2017)
and several second-order benchmarks
produced by our generator. We next present our test setting and discuss our
findings. The implementation of our algorithms, the datasets, and the raw
output produced by our system are available
online.
2
2

2

https://krr-nas.cs.ox.ac.uk/2026/goal-driven-QA/

5.1.
Test Setting

Our prototype can load a base instance and a set of dependencies, and either
compute the chase in full, or answer a specific query by applying our relevance
analysis and/or magic sets algorithms. The system was written in Java. We used
the API by
Benedikt et al. (2017)
for loading and manipulating
instances and dependencies, and we implemented our algorithms on top of this
API exactly as specified in Section
4
. In the magic sets
algorithm, a SIPS is produced by a greedy algorithm that eagerly binds as many
variables as possible while avoiding cross-products or violating the
requirements of Definition
18
.

We computed the fixpoint of a logic program and the chase of first- and
second-order dependencies with equality using a research version of the RDFox
system. RDFox was implemented in C++, but it provides a Java API. The system
can be configured to treat
\approx
as either ordinary or ‘true’ equality, and
in either case it can efficiently parallelise the fixpoint computation for a
set of rules written in an extension of Datalog

(
Motik et al., 2014
;
Motik et al., 2015b
)
. While
RDFox does not implement the chase variant from
Section
3.2
natively, we used the following
techniques to overcome any discrepancies.

First, RDFox natively handles only RDF triples (i.e., facts of the form

\langle s,p,o\rangle
), so we encode facts of varying arity into triples.
In particular, we represent unary and binary facts
A(a)
and
R(a,b)
as

{\langle a,\mathit{rdf}{:}\mathit{type},A\rangle}
and
{\langle a,R,b\rangle}
, respectively. Moreover, we reify facts of higher arity; for example,
we transform
S(a,b,c)
into triples
{\langle a,S{:}1,t\rangle}
,
{\langle b,S{:}2,t\rangle}
, and
{\langle c,S{:}3,t\rangle}
, where
t
is a fresh
labelled null that is uniquely determined by the fact’s arguments, and
S{:}1
,

S{:}2
, and
S{:}3
are fresh properties that associate
t
with the
corresponding arguments of
S(a,b,c)
. The following example illustrates how
dependencies containing atoms of higher arity are translated into RDFox rules
over triples.

S(x,y,z)\rightarrow T(x,y,z)\quad\rightsquigarrow\quad

[?x,T:1,?w], [?y,T:2,?w], [?z,T:3,?w] :-

[?x,S:1,?v], [?y,S:2,?v], [?z,S:3,?v],

BIND(SKOLEM("S",?x,?y,?z) AS ?v),

BIND(SKOLEM("T",?x,?y,?z) AS ?w).

Literal
BIND(SKOLEM("S",?x,?y,?z) AS ?v)
of the rule binds the
variable
?v
to a fresh labelled null that is unique for each
instantiation of
?x
,
?y
, and
?z
.

Second, RDFox does not support function symbols natively, so we simulated them
using a builtin function. In the following rule, the
BIND
construct
assigns to
?z
a value that is uniquely computed from
f
and

?x
; hence, the value of
?z
can be seen as the result of
applying
f
to the value of
?x
.

A\rightarrow R(x,f(x))\quad\rightsquigarrow\quad

[?x,R,?z] :-

[?x,rdf:type,A], BIND(SKOLEM("f",?x) AS ?z) .

Third, RDFox does not natively provide inferences that realise functional
reflexivity. To overcome this issue, we removed all function symbols from the
rule bodies as explained in Section
4.8
; please recall
that this introduces a fresh
n+1
-ary predicate
\mathsf{F}_{f}
for each
n
-ary
(Skolem or true) function symbol
f
. Moreover, we introduced the rule

{\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y)\wedge\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y^{\prime})\rightarrow y\approx y^{\prime}}
for each true function symbol
f
in order to ensure that
f
is
interpreted as a function.

Fourth, RDFox does not handle existential quantifiers over individual variables
directly, so we Skolemised all such quantifiers before processing.

With these modifications in place, RDFox realises the chase variant described
in Section
3.2
with Skolem and true function
symbols, but no first-order existential quantification. We used this algorithm
both as a baseline and for computing the chase in
line
8
of Algorithm
1
.

We conducted all experiments on a server equipped with 256 GB RAM and an
Intel(R) Xeon(R) Silver 4116 CPU with 48 cores and clock frequency 2.10 GHz
running Ubuntu 22.04.4 LTS.

Our evaluation involves five first-order and nine second-order test scenarios
shown in Table
1
. We consider first- and second-order
scenarios separately to evaluate our techniques in the more common setting of
TGDs and EGDs, as well as on the more advanced SO dependencies. Each scenario
consists of a set of dependencies, a base instance, and a set of queries. For
SO scenarios, we slightly abuse the terminology and use the terms ‘TGD’ and
‘EGD’ to refer to dependency conjuncts with relational and equality head atoms,
respectively.

We used the first-order scenarios by
Benedikt et al. (2017)

for benchmarking chase systems. In particular,
LUBM-100
and
LUBM-1k
are
derived from the well-known Lehigh University Benchmark

(
Guo et al., 2005
)
that has extensively been used to evaluate
performance of various reasoning systems. Moreover,
Deep200
and

Deep300
are synthetic benchmarks designed to stress-test chase systems:
universal models are large despite the base instance being quite small.
Finally, the
Ont-256
scenario is a synthetic benchmark designed to test reasoning
with equality-generating dependencies.

We produced second-order dependencies using our generator from
Appendix
F
parameterised as follows; please refer to the
appendix for a discussion of the various parameters. We used
N_{\mathsf{PA}}=2
,

N_{\mathsf{FA}}=1
,
N_{\mathsf{P}}=456,976
,
N_{\mathsf{FS}}=17,576
,
N_{\mathsf{C}}=10,000
,

N_{\mathsf{Q}}=120
, and
N_{\mathsf{D}}=5
in all cases, and we produced three
sets of dependencies using the following parameters.

•

Gen-26
:

N_{\mathsf{R}}=26
,
N_{\mathsf{F}}=2
,

N_{\mathsf{RA}}=3
, and
N_{\mathsf{EQ}}=1
.

•

Gen-27
:

N_{\mathsf{R}}=27
,
N_{\mathsf{F}}=2
,

N_{\mathsf{RA}}=4
, and
N_{\mathsf{EQ}}=2
.

•

Gen-28
:

N_{\mathsf{R}}=28
,
N_{\mathsf{F}}=1
,

N_{\mathsf{RA}}=3
, and
N_{\mathsf{EQ}}=1
.

For each of these three sets, we generated three accompanying base instances by
using
N_{\mathsf{T}}
as 1k, 10k, and 50k. We thus obtained a total of nine
second-order test scenarios shown in Table
1
.

For each scenario, we computed the chase, and we answered each query as shown
in Algorithm
1
using just the relevance analysis, just the
magic sets transformation, or using both techniques. When computing the chase
or using just the relevance analysis, the rules did not contain functional
terms in relational body atoms, so we skipped the elimination of function
symbols (see Section
4.7
). In each case we measured the
wall-clock processing time (i.e., the time needed to compute the chase or to
run Algorithm
1
); this excludes the time needed to load
the base instance, but it includes the time needed to run the relevance
analysis (Algorithm
2
) and the magic sets transformation
(Algorithm
3
). We also recorded the number of rules in the
program
P_{7}
in line
7
of
Algorithm
1
, and the numbers of all and ‘useful’ facts
(i.e., facts not involving any auxiliary predicates such as
\mathsf{D}
,
\mathsf{F}_{f}
,
or magic predicates) in the instance
I
in line
8
.

Figure 5.
The results for

,

,

, and

on first-order scenarios

Figure
5
shows the distributions of our results on
first-order scenarios using cactus plots: the horizontal axis enumerates all
queries sorted by the value being shown, and the vertical axis shows the
corresponding value (i.e., the query time in milliseconds, the number of
derived facts, and the number of the rules in the program
P_{7}
). In each
figure,
Rel
(shown in blue),
Mag
(shown in green), and
Rel+Mag
(shown in red) refer to the variant of Algorithm
1
that
uses the relevance analysis only, the magic sets only, and both techniques in
combination, respectively. Furthermore,
Mat
(shown in black) provides the
baseline of computing the chase in full, which is equivalent to using
Algorithm
1
with no relevance analysis or magic sets:
singularisation and desingularisation then cancel each other out, and
Skolemisation and elimination of function symbols in the body are already
required by the RDFox-based chase computation procedure described in
Section
5.1
. Finally, Table
2

summarises the distributions by showing the minimum, maximum, and median
values. Computing the chase in full for
Deep300
was aborted after two
hours.

As one can see, our techniques are generally very effective: a query can
sometimes be answered orders of magnitude faster than by computing the chase in
full. In fact, goal-driven query answering can mean the difference between
success and failure: whereas
Mat
failed for
Deep300
, we were able to
successfully answer all queries of this scenario. This may not seem surprising:
both relevance analysis and magic sets identify a subset of the relevant rules,
and so computing the chase in line
8
of
Algorithm
1
should intuitively be more efficient than
computing the chase in full. However, a more detailed analysis of our results
reveals a much more nuanced picture, as we discuss next.

Relevance analysis always returns a subset of the input rules, so one may
naturally expect it to be always faster than computing the chase in full.
However, the query times for
Rel
are the same for most queries of

Deep200
and
Deep300
, which suggests that the technique contains a
fixed overhead. Indeed, our analysis revealed that, on these scenarios, the
fixpoint computation on the critical instance in
line
2
of Algorithm
2

dominates the overall processing time. As a result,
Rel
is only
marginally faster than
Mat
, and considerably slower than
Mag
on most
queries. In all other cases, the overhead of relevance analysis is negligible
and the technique seems very effective.

The rules relevant to a query could be determined by a simple reachability
analysis: we identify the query predicate
\mathsf{Q}
as reachable; we
transitively propagate reachability over the rules (i.e., if a predicate
occurring in a head atom of a rule is reachable, then all predicates occurring
in the rule body are reachable too); and we select all rules containing a
reachable predicate in the head. However, as soon as the input dependencies
contain equality, rules (
2
), (
3
), and (
7
)
ensure that all predicates, and thus all rules, are reachable. In contrast, our
relevance analysis technique computes much smaller programs on the
Ont-256
scenario, suggesting that the technique is considerably more effective than
simple reachability. This is because our technique does not consider body atoms
in isolation, but instead tries to identify whether a rule has relevant
instances. This, in turn, seems to be key to an effective analysis of
inferences with equality.

The magic sets algorithm implicitly performs the reachability analysis but also
identifies relevant rule instances. This allows
Mag
to be faster than

Mat
on all scenarios apart from
Ont-256
. However, by introducing magic rules,
the algorithm can sometimes produce programs that are larger than the input,
and the evaluation of these additional rules can be a source of overhead. For
example,
Mag
is consistently slower than
Mat
on
Ont-256
, primarily
because of the magic rules. In fact, as Table
2
shows, the
ratios of the numbers of total and useful facts are orders of magnitude higher
for
Mag
than for
Mat
and
Rel
, which suggests further that
deriving magic facts can be costly.

The combination of both techniques often produces the best results (e.g., on

LUBM
and some
Ont-256
queries). Intuitively, the relevance analysis can reduce
the number of rules sufficiently so that the introduction of magic rules leads
to further improvements in query answering performance.
