Figure 7.
The results for
Mat
,
Rel
,
Mag
, and
Rel+Mag
on second-order scenarios

Figures
6
and
7
show the results of
our experiments on second-order scenarios. Since the numbers of rules are
independent of the sizes of the base instances, we show the numbers of rules
just once. Table
3
summarises the distribution of our
results.

Our results on second-order dependencies are broadly in agreement with the
results on first-order dependencies. Specifically, our techniques seem to be
effective in that they allow most queries to be answered orders of magnitude
faster than by computing the chase. The difference between magic sets and
relevance is less pronounced than in our first-order experiments: across the
board,
Mat
is slower than
Mag
, which is in turn considerably slower
than
Rel
. Moreover, the difference between
Rel
and
Rel+Mag
is negligible; thus, while the magic sets do not considerably improve
performance, they do not hurt it either.

This behaviour is explained to some extent by the very high ratios of the
numbers of total and useful facts: as shown in Table
3
,
these often exceed a factor of ten, and already
Mat
incurs considerable
overheads (unlike on first-order scenarios). We confirmed that this is mainly
due to the facts involving the
\mathsf{F}_{f}
predicates produced by

\mathsf{D}
-restricted function reflexivity axioms. In particular, as per
Definition
25
, each axiom of the form (
22
) introduces
a rule of the form (
95
), which is transformed by
desingularisation into

(111)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{f}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\leftarrow\mathsf{D}(x_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge\mathsf{D}(x_{n}).

In other words, these axioms generate the value of
f
on all values of
{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}}
that have been identified as relevant (i.e., they occur in facts
with the
\mathsf{D}
predicate, which is the case whenever they occur in relational
facts). This is a considerable source of overhead, particularly when the
relevant terms are deep. The most effective way to reduce this overhead seems
to be to remove function symbols altogether, which explains why performance
improvements of
Rel
are much more substantial than of
Mag
or

Rel+Mag
.

At present, it is not clear whether this inefficiency can be avoided. The chase
for second-order dependencies by
Arenas et al. (2011)
uses
a similar step: it enumerates the domain of the Herbrand universe up to a depth
that is determined in advance from the graph used to ensure that the input is
weakly acyclic. Our approach is somewhat more efficient in that facts with the

\mathsf{F}_{f}
predicates are produced ‘on demand’ (i.e., only for the relevant
terms).

Nevertheless, our results show that efficient goal-driven query answering over
generalised second-order dependencies is feasible and can lead to considerable
efficiency gains in practice.

6.
Conclusion

In this paper, we presented several techniques for goal-driven query answering
over first- and second-order dependencies with equality. Towards this goal, we
presented a revised formulation of the singularisation technique by

Marnette (2009)
, which overcomes the incompleteness on
second-order dependencies in the work by
ten Cate et al. (2016)
.
Furthermore, we presented a novel relevance analysis technique that tries to
identify rules whose instances are all irrelevant to the query, and we
presented a variant of the magic sets technique for logic programs

(
Bancilhon et al., 1986
;
Beeri and Ramakrishnan, 1991
;
Balbin et al., 1991
)
that can identify the relevant instances of
rules with equality. In order to evaluate our techniques on second-order
dependencies, we presented (in Appendix
F
) a technique for
generating synthetic second-order scenarios that are guaranteed to exhibit
nontrivial inferences. Finally, we conducted an extensive empirical evaluation
and showed that our techniques can often be used to answer a query orders of
magnitude faster than by computing the chase of the input dependencies in full.

We see several interesting avenues for future work. On the theoretical side, it
would be interesting to make our techniques applicable to classes of decidable
dependencies with nonterminating chase (e.g., guarded dependencies). The key
difficulty in relevance analysis is that the fixpoint of the base instance
abstraction may not always be finite. Reasoning in such cases often involves
computing finite representations of infinite models, but it is unclear how to
perform backward chaining over such representations. The key difficulty in the
magic sets is to ensure that the transformation result falls into a decidable
dependency class. On the practical side, a key challenge is to develop ways to
reduce the number of auxiliary facts (i.e., facts involving the
\mathsf{D}
,

\mathsf{F}_{f}
, or magic predicates), as well as ways to reduce the overheads of
applying magic rules.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
6

See
6

Proof.

For arbitrary
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
,
B
, and
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
as in the claim,
we show that
{\Sigma\cup B\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if

{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B\not\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. The proof of
the (
\Rightarrow
) direction is straightforward. If
{\Sigma\cup B\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, then there exists an interpretation
I
such
that
{I\models_{\approx}\Sigma\cup B}
and
{I\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. Now
let
I^{\prime}
be the interpretation obtained from
I
by simply setting
{\mathsf{D}^{I^{\prime}}=\Delta^{I^{\prime}}=\Delta^{I}}
. Predicate
\approx
is ‘true’ equality in
I
, which
clearly ensures
{I^{\prime}\models\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
and
{I^{\prime}\not\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. This implies
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B\not\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, as required.

For the (
\Leftarrow
) direction, we assume that
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B\not\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, and we show that this implies
{\Sigma\cup B\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. We achieve this goal in six steps.
First, we use a variant of the chase to construct a Herbrand interpretation

J^{\infty}
such that
{J^{\infty}\models\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
and

{J^{\infty}\not\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
; note that
\approx
is interpreted in

J^{\infty}
as an ‘ordinary’ predicate without any special meaning. Second, we
show that the interpretation of predicate
\mathsf{D}
in
J^{\infty}
satisfies a
particular property. Third, we convert
J^{\infty}
into an interpretation
I^{\prime}

that satisfies the unrestricted functional reflexivity axioms (
6
).
Fourth, we define a function
\rho
that maps each domain element of

\Delta^{I^{\prime}}
into a representative domain element, and we use
\rho
to
convert
I^{\prime}
into an interpretation
I
where predicate
\approx
is
interpreted as ‘true’ equality. Fifth, we prove that
{I\models_{\approx}\Sigma\cup B}
and
{I\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, which in turn implies
{\Sigma\cup B\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

(Step 1.)
We construct the Herbrand interpretation
J^{\infty}
using a
variant of the chase that can handle both function symbols and first-order
quantifiers, which we summarise next. We assume we have a countably infinite
set of
labelled nulls
that is pairwise disjoint with the sets of
constants, function symbols, and predicates. Labelled nulls can be used to
construct terms just like constants; for example,
f(x,c,n)
is a term
containing variable
x
, constant
c
, and labelled null
n
. Note that
B
is
a base instance and thus does not contain labelled nulls. Now let
{J^{0},J^{1},\dots}
be a sequence of instances such that
{J^{0}=B\cup\{\mathsf{D}(c)\mid\text{ constant }c\text{ occurs in }\Sigma^{\prime}\cup B\}}
and where each

J^{i+1}
with
{i\geq 0}
is obtained by extending
J^{i}
as follows.

•

For each dependency
{\forall\mathbf{x}.(\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))}
in
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
and
each substitution
\sigma
mapping
\mathbf{x}
to the terms of
J^{i}
such that

{\varphi(\mathbf{x})\sigma\subseteq J^{i}}
and no extension
\sigma^{\prime}
of

\sigma
to the variables of
\mathbf{y}
satisfies
{\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq J^{i}}
, add to
J^{i+1}
all facts of
{\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
where substitution
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
extends
\sigma
by mapping
all variables of
\mathbf{y}
to fresh labelled nulls not occurring in
J^{i}
.

•

For each fact
R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
with
R
different from
\approx

and
\mathsf{Q}
introduced in the previous step, add to
J^{i+1}
facts

{\mathsf{D}(t_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{D}(t_{n})}
.

Let
{J^{\infty}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}J^{i}}
. Like the chase for SO-TGDs

(
Fagin et al., 2005b
)
, this chase variant interprets function
symbols by themselves and introduces functional ground terms. This is in fact
the main difference from the chase variant outlined in
Section
3.2
, which interprets functional terms
using labelled nulls. The approach outlined above is possible because equality
is treated as an ordinary predicate and our objective is to construct a
Herbrand model. Furthermore, like the restricted chase

(
Fagin et al., 2005a
)
for first-order dependencies, the approach
above introduces labelled nulls to satisfy the first-order existential
quantifiers. Finally, the approach above applies axioms (
1
) and
(
2
) eagerly for convenience. By the standard properties of the
mentioned chase variants,
J^{\infty}
is a Herbrand interpretation that
satisfies
{J^{\infty}\models\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
and
{J^{\infty}\not\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

(Step 2.)
We show by induction on
{i\geq 0}
that each
J^{i}
satisfies
the following auxiliary property, which is then clearly also satisfied for
{i=\infty}
.

(
\ast
): For each (relational or equality) fact
{F\in J^{i}}
, each
argument
t
of
F
, and each proper subterm
s
of
t
, it is the case
that
{\mathsf{D}(s)\in J^{i}}
; moreover, if the predicate of
F
is different from

\approx
and
\mathsf{Q}
, then
{\mathsf{D}(t)\in J^{i}}
holds as well.

The base case
{i=0}
holds by the definition of
J^{0}
and the fact
J^{0}

contains no functional terms and no equality facts. Now assume that
J^{i}

satisfies (
\ast
) and consider an arbitrary fact
{F\in J^{i+1}\setminus J^{i}}
. Fact
F
clearly satisfies (
\ast
) if it is produced by a dependency in

\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. The only remaining possibility is that
F
is produced by a
dependency
{\delta^{\prime}=\forall\mathbf{x}^{\prime}.[\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})]}
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
by substitutions
\sigma
and

\sigma^{\prime\prime}
where
{\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})\sigma\subseteq J^{i}}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}

extends
\sigma
by mapping all variables of
\mathbf{y}
to fresh labelled nulls.
Dependency
\delta^{\prime}
can be one of the following two forms.

•

Assume that
\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})
does not contain the query
predicate
\mathsf{Q}
. Dependency
\delta^{\prime}
is obtained from a safe dependency

{\delta\in\Sigma}
by singularisation, which does not affect safety.
Thus, each variable
{x\in\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
occurs as an argument of a relational
atom of
\varphi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, and this atom is matched in the instance
J^{i}

to a relational fact satisfying (
\ast
); thus,
x\sigma
occurs as an
argument of a relational fact in
J^{i}
, and
{\mathsf{D}(s)\in J^{i}\subseteq J^{i+1}}
holds for each subterm
s
of
x\sigma
. Thus, fact
F
is
produced by instantiating a relational or an equality atom of
\psi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})
of the form
R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
(where
R
can be
\approx
).
Each term
t_{i}
is of depth at most one, so
{\mathsf{D}(s)\in J^{i}\subseteq J^{i+1}}
holds for each proper subterm
s
of
t_{i}\sigma
. If
{R={\approx}}
, fact
F
clearly satisfies (
\ast
). If
{R\neq{\approx}}
,
then our definition of the chase ensures
{\mathsf{D}(t_{i}\sigma)\in J^{i}\subseteq J^{i+1}}
for each
t_{i}
, so
F
again satisfies (
\ast
).

•

The only remaining possibility is that
\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})
is a
query atom for the form
\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})
, in which case

\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
contains
{x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{n}\approx x_{n}^{\prime}}
. Now each atom
{x_{i}\approx x_{i}^{\prime}}
is matched in
J^{i}
to
an equality fact that satisfies (
\ast
), so
{\mathsf{D}(s)\in J^{i}}
holds each
proper subterm
s
of
x_{i}^{\prime}\sigma
. Clearly, fact
{F=\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
satisfies (
\ast
).

(Step 3.)
Our objective is to convert the Herbrand interpretation

J^{\infty}
into an interpretation
I
that interprets
\approx
as ‘true
equality’, and we shall achieve this by replacing all terms in
J^{\infty}
that
belong to the same equivalence class in
J^{\infty}
with a representative.
However, interpretation
J^{\infty}
satisfies only the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted
functional reflexivity axioms (
22
), but not their unrestricted
version. As a result,
J^{\infty}
can contain a fact
{s\approx t}
without
also containing
{f(s)\approx f(t)}
, which makes choosing the representatives
while satisfying unrestricted functional reflexivity difficult. We overcome
this by first converting
J^{\infty}
into an auxiliary interpretation
I^{\prime}
where
we ‘remap’ each term not occurring in a relational fact into a fresh, distinct
domain element
\omega
. This ensures that
I^{\prime}
satisfies both
{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
and the unrestricted functional reflexivity axioms.

In particular, let
T
be the set of all terms occurring in a (relational or
equality) fact of
J^{\infty}
, and let
\omega
be a fresh labelled null not
occurring in
T
. The
\approx
predicate is reflexive, symmetric, and
transitive in
J^{\infty}
so it partitions the ground terms of
J^{\infty}
into
equivalence classes. For each equivalence class
C
, we select a representative
for
C
as follows: select arbitrary
{s\in C}
such that
{\mathsf{D}(s)\in J^{\infty}}
if one exists, and select arbitrary
{s\in C}
otherwise. For each
ground term
t
occurring in
J^{\infty}
, let
\hat{t}
be a representative for
the equivalence class that
t
belongs to. Finally, let
{\hat{\omega}=\omega}
.

Interpretation
I^{\prime}
is defined as shown below, where
c
ranges over all
constants,
f
ranges over all function symbols,
n
is the arity of
f
, tuple

{\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle}
ranges over

\big(\Delta^{I^{\prime}}\big)^{n}
,
R
ranges over relational predicates, and
m
is
the arity of
R
.

\displaystyle\Delta^{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=T\cup\{\omega\}

\displaystyle c^{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=c

\displaystyle f^{I^{\prime}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})&\text{if }f(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in T\\
f(\hat{\alpha_{1}},\dots,\hat{\alpha_{n}})&\text{if }f(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\not\in T\text{ and }\mathsf{D}(\hat{\alpha_{i}})\in J^{\infty}\text{ for each }i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\\
\omega&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle R^{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle\mid R(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})\in J^{\infty}\}

\displaystyle\approx^{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle\mid t_{1}\approx t_{2}\in J^{\infty}\}\cup\{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\}

To see that
{I^{\prime}\models\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})\cup B}
holds, consider an
arbitrary generalised first-order dependency
{\delta^{\prime}=\forall\mathbf{x}^{\prime}.[\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})]}
in

{\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
and a valuation
\pi^{\prime}
such that
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}
. If
{\delta^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SG}(\Sigma^{\prime})}
, then
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\delta^{\prime}}
because
{\mathsf{D}^{I^{\prime}}=\mathsf{D}^{J^{\infty}}}
and
\omega
is not
connected in
\approx^{I^{\prime}}
to any element of
T
. Now assume that
\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})
does not contain the query predicate
\mathsf{Q}
. For each variable

x
in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, variable
x
occurs in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
in a
relational atom since
\delta^{\prime}
is safe, and
{\pi^{\prime}(x)\in T}
since no tuple
in any
R^{I^{\prime}}
contains
\omega
. Thus,
{J^{\infty},\pi^{\prime}\models\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})}
clearly implies
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})}
. Furthermore, assume that
\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})

is of the form
\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})
, so
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
is of the form

{\phi\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{n}\approx x_{n}^{\prime}}
. Formula

\phi
satisfies the safety condition so
{\pi^{\prime}(x)\in T}
holds for each
variable
x
in
\phi
; moreover,
\omega
is not connected in
\approx^{I^{\prime}}

to any element of
T
, so
{\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})\in T}
for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
; thus, we have
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})}
. Finally, we
show that
I^{\prime}
satisfies the unrestricted functional reflexivity axiom
(
6
) for each
n
-ary function symbol
f
. To this end, we consider
arbitrary valuation
\pi^{\prime}
such that
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{n}\approx x_{n}^{\prime}}
, and we show
{\langle f^{I^{\prime}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n}),f^{I^{\prime}}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\rangle\in{\approx}^{I^{\prime}}}
, where
{u_{i}=\pi^{\prime}(x_{i})}

and
{v_{i}=\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})}
for each
{i\in N}
and
{N=\{1,\dots,n\}}
.

•

Assume that
{\{\mathsf{D}(\hat{u}_{i}),\mathsf{D}(\hat{v}_{i})\}\subseteq J^{\infty}}

for each
{i\in N}
. If
{f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\in T}
, let
{u_{i}^{\prime}=u_{i}}
for
each
{i\in N}
; then, property (
\ast
) ensures
{\{\mathsf{D}(u_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,\mathsf{D}(u_{n}^{\prime})\}\subseteq J^{\infty}}
. If
{f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\not\in T}
, let

{u_{i}^{\prime}=\hat{u}_{i}}
for each
{i\in N}
; then,
{\mathsf{D}(u_{i}^{\prime})\in J^{\infty}}

for each
{i\in N}
holds by our assumption. Analogously, if

{f(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in T}
, let
{v_{i}^{\prime}=v_{i}}
for each
{i\in N}
, and
otherwise let
{v_{i}^{\prime}=\hat{v}_{i}}
; we again have
{\mathsf{D}(v_{i}^{\prime})\in J^{\infty}}

for each
{i\in N}
. These definitions clearly ensure

{f^{I^{\prime}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})=f(u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{n}^{\prime})}
and
{f^{I^{\prime}}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=f(v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{n}^{\prime})}
, as well as
{u_{i}^{\prime}\approx v_{i}^{\prime}\in J^{\infty}}
for
each
{i\in N}
. But then, the
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity
axiom (
22
) for
f
ensures
{f(u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{n}^{\prime})\approx f(v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{n}^{\prime})\in J^{\infty}}
Consequently, we have
{\langle f(u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{n}^{\prime}),f(v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{n}^{\prime})\rangle\in{\approx}^{I^{\prime}}}
, as
required.

•

Assume that there exists
{i\in N}
such that either
{\mathsf{D}(\hat{u}_{i})\not\in J^{\infty}}
or
{\mathsf{D}(\hat{v}_{i})\not\in J^{\infty}}
. Since
\hat{u}_{i}

and
\hat{v}_{i}
are in the same equivalence class, we have
{\{\mathsf{D}(\hat{u}_{i}),\mathsf{D}(\hat{v}_{i})\}\cap J^{\infty}=\emptyset}
. But then,
property (
\ast
) and the definition of representatives ensure

{f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\not\in T}
and
{f(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\not\in T}
. Thus,
the definition of
f^{I^{\prime}}
ensures
{f^{I^{\prime}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})=f^{I^{\prime}}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=\omega}
, and so
{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\in{\approx}^{I^{\prime}}}
, as required.

(Step 4.)
Dependencies (
3
), (
4
), and
(
5
) ensure that
\approx^{I^{\prime}}
is an equivalence relation on

\Delta^{I^{\prime}}
. Let
{\rho:\Delta^{I^{\prime}}\to\Delta^{I^{\prime}}}
be an arbitrary
function that maps each
{\alpha\in\Delta^{I^{\prime}}}
to an element
{\rho(\alpha)\in\Delta^{I^{\prime}}}
that is unique associated with the equivalence class of

\alpha
—that is, for all
{\alpha,\beta\in\Delta^{I^{\prime}}}
we have

{\rho(\alpha)=\rho(\beta)}
if and only if
\alpha
and
\beta
belong to
the same equivalence class. The fact that
I^{\prime}
satisfies all equality
dependencies including the unrestricted functional reflexivity ensures the
following property.

(
\blacklozenge
): For all
{\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}\}\subseteq\Delta^{I^{\prime}}}
such that
{\rho(\alpha_{i})=\rho(\beta_{i})}
for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
, we have

{\rho(f^{I^{\prime}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}))=\rho(f^{I^{\prime}}(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}))}
.

We use
\rho
to convert
I^{\prime}
into an interpretation
I
where
\approx
is
interpreted as ‘true’ equality as follows, where
c
ranges over all constants,

f
ranges over all function symbols,
n
is the arity of
f
, tuple
{\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle}
ranges over
\big(\Delta^{I}\big)^{n}
,
R

ranges over relational predicates, and
m
is the arity of
R
.

\displaystyle\Delta^{I}

\displaystyle=\{\rho(\alpha)\mid\alpha\in\Delta^{I^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle c^{I}

\displaystyle=\rho(c^{I^{\prime}})

\displaystyle R^{I}

\displaystyle=\{\langle\rho(\alpha_{1}),\dots,\rho(\alpha_{m})\rangle\mid\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}\rangle\in R^{I^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle f^{I}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=\rho(f^{I^{\prime}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}))

\displaystyle\approx^{I}

\displaystyle=\{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle\mid\alpha\in\Delta^{I}\}

(Step 5.)
It is obvious that
{I\models_{\approx}B}
, and we next prove
{I\models_{\approx}\Sigma}
and
{I\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. The construction of

I
ensures that
I
satisfies the properties of equality. Now consider an
arbitrary generalised FO dependency
{\delta=\forall\mathbf{x}.[\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]\in\Sigma}
, its
singularisation
{\delta^{\prime}=\forall\mathbf{x}^{\prime}.[\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})]\in\Sigma^{\prime}}
, and a valuation
\pi

such that
{I,\pi\models_{\approx}\varphi(\mathbf{x})}
. We define a valuation
\pi^{\prime}
on

I^{\prime}
as follows.

•

For each relational atom
R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
and
the corresponding atom
R(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})
in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, choose
any tuple
{\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle\in R^{I^{\prime}}}
such
that
{\rho(\alpha_{i})=t_{i}^{I,\pi}}
for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
,
and define
{\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})=\alpha_{i}}
for each such
i
. This definition is
correctly formed since
{I,\pi\models_{\approx}R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})}
and the
construction of
I
guarantee the existence of at least one such
{\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle}
, and singularisation ensures that each

x_{i}^{\prime}
occurs exactly once in exactly one relational atom of
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
.

•

Due to safety, each variable
x^{\prime}
in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
not covered
by the previous case necessarily occurs in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
in a unique
equality atom of the form
{x\approx x^{\prime}}
that is introduced because

\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
contains the query predicate
\mathsf{Q}
. We define

{\pi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\pi^{\prime}(x)}
, and we call such
x^{\prime}
a
query variable
.

Interpretation
I
and valuation
\pi^{\prime}
satisfy the following auxiliary
property.

(
\lozenge
): For each term
t
occurring in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
, we have

{t^{I,\pi}=\rho(t^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})}
.

If
t
is a variable
x
occurring in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
, the definition of

\pi^{\prime}
clearly ensures
{\pi(x)=\rho(\pi^{\prime}(x))}
. Moreover, if
t
is a
constant
c
, the definition of
I
ensures
{c^{I,\pi}=\rho(c^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})}
.
Finally, if
t
is of the form
{t=f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})}
where each
t_{i}
is a
constant or a variable occurring in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
, we get the required
property as follows, where the second equality holds by the definition of
f^{I}

and the fact that
{t_{i}^{I,\pi}=\rho(t_{i}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})}
for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
, and the third equality holds due to property (
\blacklozenge
).

\displaystyle t^{I,\pi}=f^{I}(t_{1}^{I,\pi},\dots,t_{n}^{I,\pi})=\rho(f^{I^{\prime}}(\rho(t_{1}^{I,\pi}),\dots,\rho(t_{n}^{I,\pi})))=\rho(f^{I^{\prime}}(t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}},\dots,t_{n}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}))=\rho(t^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})

We are now ready to show that
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}
. The first item in the definition of
\pi^{\prime}
clearly ensures
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models R(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})}
for each relational atom in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
.
We next show that
I^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime}
satisfy each equality of
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
.
Note that singularisation changes neither the equality atoms nor the functional
terms occurring in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
.

•

Consider any equality atom
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

that also occurs in
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
. Then,
{I,\pi\models_{\approx}t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
implies
{t_{1}^{I,\pi}=t_{2}^{I,\pi}}
, and property (
\lozenge
)
ensures
{\rho(t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})=t_{1}^{I,\pi}}
and
{\rho(t_{2}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})=t_{2}^{I,\pi}}
. Hence, we have
{\rho(t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})=\rho(t_{2}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}})}
, so terms
t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
and
t_{2}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
belong
to the same equivalence class of
\approx^{I^{\prime}}
by the definition of

\rho
; thus,
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
holds, as required.

•

Each equality atom in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
not occurring in

\varphi(\mathbf{x})
is of the form
{x_{i}^{\prime}=t_{i}}
and was obtained by
applying a singularisation step to the argument
t_{i}
of a relational atom

R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
; moreover,
R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
is eventually reduced to a
relational atom
R(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})
in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
. We have the
following possibilities for
t_{i}
.

–

Assume
t_{i}
is a variable
z_{k}
occurring in another relational
atom
{S(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})}
of
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
. Formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
then contains a relational atom
{S(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})}
where
{t_{i}=u_{k}=z_{k}}
. But then, the definition of
\pi^{\prime}
ensures
{\pi(z_{k})=\rho(\pi^{\prime}(z_{k}))=\rho(\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime}))}
, and the definition of
\rho

ensures
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models x_{i}^{\prime}\approx z_{k}}
, as required.

–

Assume
t_{i}
is a constant. The definition of
\pi^{\prime}
and
I

ensure
{\rho(\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime}))=t_{i}^{I,\pi}}
. Thus,
\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})
and

t_{i}^{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
belong to the same equivalence class of
\approx^{I^{\prime}}

by the definition of
\rho
, and
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models x_{i}^{\prime}\approx t_{i}}

holds, as required.

–

Assume
t_{i}
is a query variable. The definition of
\pi^{\prime}
then
ensures
{\pi^{\prime}(t_{i})=\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})}
, which in turn ensures
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models x_{i}^{\prime}\approx t_{i}}
, as required.

Since
{I^{\prime}\models\Sigma^{\prime}}
by our initial assumption,
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}
implies
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})}
, so there exists a valuation
\xi^{\prime}
obtained by extending
\pi^{\prime}
to
the variables of
\mathbf{y}
such that
{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}\models\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})}
;
since
\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y})
is already satisfied in

J^{\infty}
, we can safely assume that the range of
\xi^{\prime}
does not contain the
special labelled null
\omega
. Let
\xi
be the valuation obtained by
extending
\pi
so that
{\xi(y)=\rho(\xi^{\prime}(y))}
for each variable
{y\in\mathbf{y}}
. Now consider an arbitrary argument
t
of a relational or equality
atom in
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. If
t
is a variable in
\mathbf{y}
, the
definition of
\xi^{\prime}
ensures
{t^{I,\xi}=\rho(t^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}})}
. Alternatively,

t
is a term of depth at most one constructed from constants, variables in

\mathbf{x}
, and function symbols, so property (
\lozenge
) and the definition of

\xi
ensure
{t^{I,\xi}=\rho(t^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}})}
. We next prove
{I,\xi\models_{\approx}\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}
by considering all possible forms of an atom in

{\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}
.

•

Consider an arbitrary relational atom
R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
in

\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Then
{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}\models R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})}
implies

{\langle t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}},\dots,t_{n}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}\rangle\in R^{I^{\prime}}}
, and so
the definition of
I
ensures
{\langle\rho(t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}),\dots,\rho(t_{n}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}})\rangle\in R^{I}}
. Finally,
{\rho(t_{i}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}})=t_{i}^{I,\xi}}
holds for each
i
, so
{\langle t_{1}^{I,\xi},\dots,t_{n}^{I,\xi}\rangle\in R^{I}}
, which ensures
{I,\xi\models_{\approx}R(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})}
, as required.

•

Consider an equality atom
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
in
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Then
{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}\models t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
ensures that
t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}

and
t_{2}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}
belong to the same equivalence class of
\approx^{I^{\prime}}
,
so
{\rho(t_{1}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}})=\rho(t_{2}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}})}
. Moreover, we again have

{\rho(t_{i}^{I^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}})=t_{i}^{I,\xi}}
for each
{i\in\{1,2\}}
, which
implies
{t_{1}^{I,\xi}=t_{2}^{I,\xi}}
. Thus,
{I,\xi\models_{\approx}t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
, as required.

We have thus shown that
{I,\pi\models_{\approx}\forall\mathbf{x}.[\varphi(\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\exists\mathbf{y}.\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]}
. This entire argument holds
for each valuation
\pi
and each generalised first-order dependency in

\Sigma
, so
{I\models_{\approx}\Sigma\cup B}
, as required.

We finally show
{I\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. Assume for contradiction
that
{I\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
for
{\mathbf{a}=a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}
, so

{\langle a_{1}^{I},\dots,a_{n}^{I}\rangle\in\mathsf{Q}^{I}}
. By the definition of
I
,
there exists a tuple
{\langle\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\rangle\in\mathsf{Q}^{I^{\prime}}}
such that
{\rho(\alpha_{i})=a_{i}^{I}}
for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
. The construction of
J^{\infty}
, the fact that the query
predicate
\mathsf{Q}
never occurs in the
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, and that fact that
the interpretations of
\mathsf{Q}
in
J^{\infty}
and
I^{\prime}
coincide ensure that

J^{\infty}
and
I^{\prime}
are minimal with respect to
\mathsf{Q}
—that is, there exist
a valuation
\pi^{\prime}
on
I^{\prime}
and a dependency in
\Sigma^{\prime}
of the form
{\delta^{\prime}=\forall\mathbf{x}^{\prime}.[\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\wedge\phi\rightarrow\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})]}
where
{\phi=x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{n}\approx x_{n}^{\prime}}
such that
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\wedge\phi}

and
{\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})=\alpha_{i}}
. Thus,
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}\models\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\wedge\phi}
and
{\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})=\alpha_{i}}
for each
i
. Now let
\pi^{\prime\prime}
be the
valuation that coincides with
\pi^{\prime}
on all variables apart from

{x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime}}
where
{\pi^{\prime\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})=a_{i}^{I^{\prime}}}
for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
. Variables
{x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime}}
do not occur in
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, which ensures
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime}\models\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}
. Moreover, for each

i
, elements
\pi^{\prime\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime})
and
\pi^{\prime}(x_{i})
belong to the same equivalence
class of
\Delta^{I^{\prime}}
, so we have
{\rho(\pi^{\prime\prime}(x_{i}^{\prime}))=\rho(\pi^{\prime}(x_{i}))=a_{i}^{I}}
; this, in turn, implies
{I^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime}\models\phi}
. But then, we have

{I^{\prime}\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, which contradicts our initial assumption

{J^{\infty}\not\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
and the observation that
{\mathsf{Q}^{I^{\prime}}=\mathsf{Q}^{J^{\infty}}}
. Thus,
{I\not\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
holds, as
required.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
14

Before proving Theorem
14
, we first address a technical
detail: the relevant program
\mathcal{R}
produced by Algorithm
2
may
contain fewer true function symbols than the input program
P
; thus,
\mathsf{FR}(\mathcal{R})

may not contain all
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity rules of
\mathsf{FR}(P)
,
and it is not obvious that the rules in
{\mathsf{FR}(P)\setminus\mathsf{FR}(\mathcal{R})}
are not
relevant to the query answers. The following lemma establishes a property that
we use later to show that the rules in
{\mathsf{FR}(P)\setminus\mathsf{FR}(\mathcal{R})}
are indeed
irrelevant.

Lemma B.1.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}
be the final sets
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{R}
obtained by
applying Algorithm
2
to a well-formed program
P
and a
base instance
B
. Then, for each function symbol
f
occurring in a fact
of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, there exists a rule
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
that contains
f
.

Proof.

Transformations in
lines
13
–
16
do not remove
function symbols, so it suffices to prove an analogous claim for the
intermediate set of rules
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
just before
line
13
. The query predicate
\mathsf{Q}
does not occur
in a rule body so facts of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{t})
are added to set

\mathcal{D}
only in line
3
; thus, no fact in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}

with the
\mathsf{Q}
predicate contains a function symbol. We next define a notion
of a function symbol
f
being
relevant
for a fact
F
. In particular,
if
F
is relational, then
f
is relevant for
F
if
f
occurs in an argument
of
F
. Furthermore, if
F
is of the form
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}
, then
f
is
relevant for
F
if (i) the outermost symbols (i.e., the constants or the
top-level function symbols) of
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are different and there exists

{i\in\{1,2\}}
such that
f
occurs in
t_{i}
, or (ii)
{t_{1}\approx t_{2}}

is of the form
{g(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\approx g(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})}
for some function
symbol
g
(which may or may not be
f
) and there exists
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
such that
f
is relevant for the fact
{u_{i}\approx v_{i}}
.

Next, we show that, for each function symbol that is relevant for a fact of

\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, set
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
contains a rule with that function symbol. Let
{I^{0},I^{1},\dots}
be the sequence of instances used to compute
{T_{P^{\prime}}^{\infty}(B^{\prime})=I}

in line
2
, and consider an arbitrary
fact of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
and an arbitrary function symbol
f
relevant for this fact.
Instance
B^{\prime}
does not contain function symbols and
{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}\subseteq I}
, so
there exist
{i>0}
and a fact
{F\in I^{i}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
such that
f
is
relevant for
F
, and, for each
{j<i}
and each fact
{F^{\prime}\in I^{j}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
, function symbol
f
is not relevant for
F^{\prime}
. But then, there exists a
rule
{r\in P^{\prime}}
and a substitution
\nu
such that
{\mathsf{b}(r)\nu\subseteq I^{i-1}}
and
{F=\mathsf{h}(r)\nu\in I^{i}\setminus I^{i-1}}
.
Line
11
ensures that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
contains each fact of

\mathsf{b}(r)\nu
apart from possibly facts of the form
{c\approx c}
. Now
f
is
clearly not relevant for facts of the form
{c\approx c}
, so our assumption
on
i
ensures that
f
is not relevant for any fact of
\mathsf{b}(r)\nu
. Thus,

r
clearly cannot be a domain rule (
1
) or (
2
), the
reflexivity rule (
3
), the symmetry rule (
4
), or the
transitivity rule (
5
) since these rules are function-free.
Moreover,
r
cannot be a
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity rule
(
22
): then
F
is of the form
{g(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\approx g(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})}
, and thus
f
is relevant for
F
only if
f
is relevant
for some
{u_{i}\approx v_{i}}
, which cannot be the case. The only remaining
possibility is
{r\in P}
. All facts in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
with the query predicate

\mathsf{Q}
consist of only constants, so the predicate of
F
is not
\mathsf{Q}
;
moreover, rule
r
is well-formed, so Definition
10
ensures
that
\mathsf{b}(r)
is well-formed too. Thus, for each variable
x
in
r
, there
exists a relational atom
{A\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
that contains
x
. Function symbol

f
is not relevant for
A\nu
by our observations thus far, which means that
no argument of
A\nu
contains
f
; but then,
x\nu
does not contain
f

either. Thus, the only way for
\mathsf{h}(r)\nu
to contain
f
is if
\mathsf{h}(r)

contains
f
. Finally, rule
r
is added to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
in
line
8
of Algorithm
2
, which
completes our claim.

We now show our main claim. Let
{\mathcal{D}^{0},\mathcal{D}^{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
be the
sequence of sets
\mathcal{D}
from Algorithm
2
where
\mathcal{D}^{0}
is
the set in line
3
, and each
\mathcal{D}^{i+1}
is obtained from

\mathcal{D}^{i}
after processing one rule
r
and one substitution
\nu
in
line
7
. We show by induction on
i
that, for each

i
, each fact
{F\in\mathcal{D}^{i}}
, and each function symbol
f
occurring in
F
,
there exists a rule
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}}
that contains
f
. The base case holds
trivially since
\mathcal{D}^{0}
does not contain function symbols. For the induction
step, assume that
\mathcal{D}^{i}
satisfies the claim, and consider a fact
{F\in\mathcal{D}^{i}}
, rule
{r\in P\cup\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(P)}
, and substitution

\nu
processed in line
7
. If
r
is the symmetry
rule (
4
) or a
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity rule
(
22
), then each fact added to
\mathcal{D}^{i+1}
in
line
11
consists of subterms of the arguments of
F
,
so
\mathcal{D}^{i+1}
satisfies the claim by the inductive hypothesis. If
r
is the
transitivity rule (
5
), the only interesting case is if term

x_{2}\nu
contains a function symbol
f
that occurs in neither
x_{1}\nu
nor

x_{3}\nu
; but then,
f
is relevant for
{x_{1}\nu\approx x_{2}\nu}
or
{x_{2}\nu\approx x_{3}\nu}
, so the claim from the previous paragraph ensures our claim.
If
r
is a domain rule (
1
), then the body of
r
is empty so

{\mathcal{D}^{i+1}=\mathcal{D}^{i}}
and the claim holds vacuously. The only remaining
possibility is
{r\in P}
or
r
is a domain rule (
2
). Now
consider an arbitrary function symbol
f
not occurring in
\mathcal{D}^{i}
that occurs
in a fact of
\mathsf{b}(r)\nu
added to
\mathcal{D}^{i+1}
in
line
11
. If
r
contains
f
, then
r
is not a domain
rule (
2
); thus,
r
is added to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
in
line
8
and the claim holds. Otherwise, rule
r
is
well-formed, so it is of one of the two forms mentioned in
Definition
10
. If
\mathsf{h}(r)
does not contain the query
predicate
\mathsf{Q}
, then
\mathsf{b}(r)
is well-formed, so each variable of
r

occurs in
\mathsf{b}(r)
in a relational atom; but then, there exists a relational
fact
{F^{\prime}\in\mathsf{b}(r)\nu}
such that
f
is relevant for
F^{\prime}
, so the previous
paragraph ensures that
f
occurs in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
. Finally, if
\mathsf{h}(r)
is of the
form
{\mathsf{Q}(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})}
, then
\mathsf{b}(r)
is of the form
{\phi\wedge x_{1}\approx x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{n}\approx x_{n}^{\prime}}
. Since
\phi
is
well-formed, the facts in
\phi\nu
are handled as above. Moreover, each fact
involving
\mathsf{Q}
in
\mathcal{D}^{i}
consists of constants only, so
x_{i}^{\prime}\nu
is a
constant for each
{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
. Thus, if
f
occurs in some fact

{x_{i}\approx x_{i}^{\prime}}
added to
\mathcal{D}^{i+1}
in line
11
,
then
f
is relevant for
{x_{i}\approx x_{i}^{\prime}}
so the previous paragraph ensures
that
f
occurs in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the correctness of our relevance analysis algorithm.

See
14

Proof.

Consider a run of Algorithm
2
on a program
P
and a base
instance
B
. Let
B^{\prime}
be the base instance used in
line
1
; let
\eta
be any mapping of constants to
constants such that
{\eta(B^{\prime})\subseteq B}
and
{\eta(c)=c}
for each
constant
c
occurring in
P
; let
I
and
P^{\prime}
be as in
line
2
; let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
be the intermediate
set of rules just before line
13
; and let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}

and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
be the final sets. For
t
a term, let
\eta(t)
be the result of
replacing in
t
each occurrence of constant
c
on which
\eta
is defined
with
\eta(c)
. The algorithm ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\subseteq P}
, so set
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}
is
well-formed; moreover, the transformation in line
16

clearly preserves this property. Note that
{\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})=\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})\subseteq\mathsf{SG}(P)}
and
{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}\subseteq I}
. Also, the query predicate
\mathsf{Q}
does not
occur in a rule body so facts of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{t})
are added to set

\mathcal{D}
only in line
3
; thus, no fact with the

\mathsf{Q}
predicate occurring in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
contains a function symbol.

(
\Leftarrow
) Let
{I^{0},I^{1},\dots}
be the sequence of instances used to
compute
T_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})}^{\infty}(B)
as described in
Section
2
. We show by induction on
i
that, for each
fact
{F\in I^{i}}
, we have
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models F}
; then,

{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\subseteq P}
and
{\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\subseteq\mathsf{SG}(P)}
clearly imply
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models F}
. The base case is trivial since
{I^{0}=B}
. For
the induction step, we assume that the claim holds for
I^{i}
, and we consider a
fact
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{i+1}\setminus I^{i}}
derived by a rule
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})}
and a substitution
\sigma
satisfying
{\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma\subseteq I^{i}}
. The inductive assumption then ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma}
. If
{r\in\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})}
, then
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma}
holds trivially; hence, we next
consider the case when
{r\not\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}}
and
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
. Then, rule
r

is obtained from some rule
{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}}
by transformations in
line
16
. Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the extension of
\sigma

such that
{\sigma^{\prime}(x)=t\sigma}
for each variable
x
that was replaced with
a term
t
. Each body atom
{A^{\prime}\in\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})}
that was not removed in
line
16
corresponds to some
{A\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
that
satisfies
{A^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}=A\sigma\in I^{i}}
. Now consider an arbitrary atom
{A^{\prime}\in\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})}
of the form
{x\approx t}
or
{t\approx x}
that was removed
in line
16
. The definition of
\sigma^{\prime}
ensures

{x\sigma^{\prime}=t\sigma^{\prime}}
. Moreover, the condition in
line
15
ensures that
\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})
contains a
relational atom
{R(\dots,x,\dots)\in\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})}
; this atom is relational, so
it was not removed in line
16
and thus satisfies

{R(\dots,x,\dots)\sigma^{\prime}\in I^{i}}
; hence,
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models R(\dots,x,\dots)\sigma^{\prime}}
holds by the inductive assumption. Moreover,

\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})
contains the domain rules (
2
) for
R
, so
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models\mathsf{D}(x)\sigma^{\prime}}
; moreover,
\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})
also
contains the reflexivity rule (
3
), so
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models x\sigma^{\prime}\approx x\sigma^{\prime}}
. Finally,
{x\sigma^{\prime}=t\sigma^{\prime}}
ensures

{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models A^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}
. This holds for each atom

{A^{\prime}\in\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})}
that was removed in line
16
, so

{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime}}
holds. Thus, we have

{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime}}
, and the definition
of
\sigma^{\prime}
ensures
{\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime}=\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma}
. Hence, we have

{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}})\cup B\models\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma}
, as required.

(
\Rightarrow
) Let
{I^{0},I^{1},\dots}
be the sequence of instances used to
compute
T_{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}^{\infty}(B)
as described in
Section
2
. We prove the claim in three steps.

Step 1.
We show by induction on
i
that
{\eta(I^{i})\subseteq I}

holds. For the base case, we have
{I^{0}=\eta(B^{\prime})\subseteq I}
, as required.
For the induction step, assume that
{\eta(I^{i})\subseteq I}
holds, and
consider a fact
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{i+1}\setminus I^{i}}
derived by a rule

{r\in P^{\prime}}
and a substitution
\sigma
satisfying
{\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma\subseteq I^{i}}
. Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the substitution defined as
{\sigma^{\prime}(x)=\eta(\sigma(x))}
on each variable
x
from the domain of
\sigma
. Since

\eta
is identity on the constants of
r
, we clearly have
{\eta(r\sigma)=r\sigma^{\prime}}
. But then, the inductive assumption ensures
{\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma^{\prime}=\eta(\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma)\subseteq\eta(I^{i})\subseteq I}
, which implies

{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma^{\prime}=\eta(\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma)\in I}
, as required.

Step 2.
Let
{\mathcal{R}^{*}=\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{R}\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})}
, and
let us say that a fact
F
is
\eta
-relevant
if
{\eta(F)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
,
and
F
is not of the form
{t\approx t}
for some term
t
. We prove by
induction on
i
that, for each
\eta
-relevant fact
{F\in I^{i}}
, we have

{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models F}
. Note that
\mathcal{R}^{*}
and
\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})

differ only in the use of
\mathsf{D}(P)
and
\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})
, respectively; we deal
with this detail in the third step.

The induction base holds trivially. Now assume that
I^{i}
satisfies this
property and consider an arbitrary rule
{r\in P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}
and
substitution
\sigma
such that
{\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma\subseteq I^{i}}
,

{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{i+1}\setminus I^{i}}
, and
\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma
is

\eta
-relevant. Thus,
\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma
is not of the form
{t\approx t}
, so

r
is not the reflexivity rule (
3
), and we have
{r\in P\cup\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(P)}
. Moreover,
{\eta(\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}}

ensures that fact
{\eta(\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma)}
was added to
\mathcal{T}
in
line
11
, so the fact was extracted from
\mathcal{T}

at some point in line
6
. Furthermore,

{\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma\subseteq I^{i}}
,
{\eta(I^{i})\subseteq I}
, and the
observation that
\eta
is identity on the constants of
P
ensures that
r

and substitution
\nu
satisfying
{r\nu=\eta(r\sigma)}
were considered in
line
7
; thus, line
8

ensures
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(P)}
. Moreover,
\eta

does not affect the function symbols, so each function symbol of

\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma
occurs in
\eta(\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma)
; thus,
Lemma
B.1
ensures
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}}
.
Line
11
ensures that each atom of
\mathsf{b}(r)\nu
not of
the form
{c\approx c}
is contained in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
. We next prove the following
property.

(
\ast
):
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma
.

First, consider a ground atom
{F\in\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma}
not of the form
{t\approx t}
. If atom
F
is relational, or if
F
contains function symbols, or
if
{P\cup B}
does not satisfy UNA, then
\eta(F)
is not of the form
{c\approx c}
for
c
a constant; hence,
\eta(F)
is added to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
in
line
11
, so the inductive assumption ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models F}
.

Second, consider a ground atom
{t\approx t\in\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma}
obtained by
instantiating some atom
{A\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
—that is,
{A\sigma=t\approx t}
. If
r
is the symmetry rule (
4
), then
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma=t\approx t}
, which contradicts
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{i+1}\setminus I^{i}}
. If

r
is the transitivity rule (
5
), then let
A^{\prime}
be the other atom
from
\mathsf{b}(r)
; regardless of how we choose
A
and
A^{\prime}
, we have
{A^{\prime}\sigma\in I^{i}}
, and
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma=A^{\prime}\sigma}
, which again contradicts

{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{i+1}\setminus I^{i}}
. Thus,
{r\not\in\mathsf{ST}}
, so the
rule
r
is well-formed. Now assume that
\mathsf{h}(r)
does not contain the

\mathsf{Q}
predicate. For each constant
c
in
A
, the domain rules in
\mathcal{R}^{*}

ensure
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{D}(c)}
, and the reflexivity rule in

\mathcal{R}^{*}
ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models c\approx c}
. Furthermore,
consider a variable
{x\in\mathsf{vars}(A)}
. Rule
r
is well-formed, so there
exists a relational atom
{A^{\prime}\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
such that
{x\in\mathsf{vars}(A^{\prime})}
. The
previous paragraph ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models A^{\prime}\sigma}
; thus,
the domain rules for the predicate of
A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{R}^{*}
ensure
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{D}(x)\sigma}
, and the reflexivity rule in
\mathcal{R}^{*}

ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models x\sigma\approx x\sigma}
. Thus, if
either argument of
A
is a variable or a constant, our observations thus far
ensure
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models A\sigma}
. Otherwise, at least one
argument of
A
contains a function symbol
f
, and
A\sigma
is of the form

{f(\mathbf{v})\approx f(\mathbf{v})}
. Our observations ensure
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{D}(v)}
and
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models v\approx v}
for
each term
{v\in\mathbf{v}}
. Moreover,
A\sigma
is not of the form
{c\approx c}
, so
\eta(A\sigma)
is added to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}
in
line
11
; but then, Lemma
B.1

ensures that
\mathsf{FR}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})
contains a
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity
rule for
f
, which in turn ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models A\sigma}
. Finally, if
\mathsf{h}(r)
contains the
\mathsf{Q}
predicate, then the
only case not considered thus far is when
A
is of the form
{x\approx x^{\prime}}

where only variable
x
occurs in a relational atom; but then, the
corresponding domain rule ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{D}(t)}
,
and the reflexivity rule in
\mathcal{R}^{*}
ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models A\sigma}
.

Thus, property (
\ast
) holds. If
{r\in\mathcal{R}^{*}}
, this immediately proves the
inductive step. Otherwise, we have
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{int}}}
, so there exists a rule
{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
obtained from
r
by transformations in
line
16
. Now consider an arbitrary body equality atom

{F\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
. If
F
is not of the form
{c\approx c}
for some constant

c
, then either
{P\cup B}
does not satisfy UNA, or
\eta(F)
is not of the
form
{c\approx c}
for
c
a constant; hence,
{\langle r,i\rangle}
is
added to
\mathcal{B}
in line
12
, so the body atom of

r^{\prime}
corresponding to
F
is not eliminated in
line
16
. Thus, we have
{\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\sigma\subseteq\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma}
, so
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma}
.

Step 3.
For each fact of the form
{\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
such that
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, line
3
makes
sure that
{\eta(\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a}))\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
; thus, the property from the
previous step ensures
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. We
now argue that
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
holds as
well. In particular, note that
{\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{*}}
, and that

{\mathcal{R}^{*}\setminus\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})}
consists of zero or more domain rules of the
form (
1
) for a constant
c
not occurring in
\mathcal{R}
, and domain
rules of the form (
2
) for a predicate
R
not occurring in
\mathcal{R}
.
Such rules can derive facts of the form
\mathsf{D}(c)
for a constant
c
occurring in
the base instance
B
in a fact with a predicate not occurring in
{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}})}
, which can eventually derive facts of the form
{c\approx c}
and

{f(\mathbf{u})\approx t}
where
\mathbf{u}
contain such constant
c
. Now consider
an arbitrary rule
{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}}
, and assume that the head of
r
does not
contain
\mathsf{Q}
. Furthermore, consider a variable
x
occurring in an equality
in
\mathsf{b}(r)
. Since
r
is well-formed, variable
x
occurs in a relational
atom
{A\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
; since
R
does not occur in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, no fact that can
be match
A
can contain constant
c
, so
x
cannot be matched to a term
containing
c
. In other words, facts
{c\approx c}
and
{f(\mathbf{u})\approx t}
mentioned earlier cannot contribute to a derivation made by
r
. Finally,
if
\mathsf{h}(r)
contains
\mathsf{Q}
, then the body of
r
consists of a well-formed
conjunction
\phi
and equalities
x\approx x^{\prime}
. Since
\phi
is well-formed,
facts of the form
{c\approx c}
and
{f(\mathbf{u})\approx t}
cannot be matched
to
\phi
as mentioned above. Furthermore, for each body equality
x\approx x^{\prime}
, variable
x
occurs in a relational atom in
\phi
so
x
cannot be
matched to a term containing
c
. Finally,
x^{\prime}
can potentially be matched to a
functional term, but then
r
does not derive a fact containing only constants.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
20

See
20

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) Each rule
{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}
where
\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})
does not contain a
magic predicate is obtained from some rule
{r\in P}
by appending an atom
with a magic predicate to the body of
r
. Furthermore, each function symbol in

\mathcal{R}
occurs in
P
, which ensures
{\mathsf{FR}(\mathcal{R})\subseteq\mathsf{FR}(P)}
. Thus, for each
fact
F
not containing a magic predicate such that
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B\models F}
, we clearly have
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models F}
.

(
\Rightarrow
) Let
{\mathcal{R}^{*}=\mathsf{D}(P)\cup\mathsf{R}\cup\mathsf{ST}\cup\mathsf{FR}(\mathcal{R})}
. We
prove in two steps that
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
implies

{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
.

Step 1.
Let
{I^{0},I^{1},\dots}
be the sequence of instances used to
compute
T_{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)}^{\infty}(B)
as specified in
Section
2
. We prove by induction on
i
that, for each
fact
{R(\mathbf{u})\in I^{i}}
not of the form
{t\approx t}
and each magic
predicate
\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}
for
R
occurring in the body of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
,
properties
(M1)
and
(M2)
hold:

(M1)

if
{\alpha={\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}
(and thus
{R={\approx}}
), then
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\cup\{\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(\mathbf{u}^{\beta})\}\models{\approx}(\mathbf{u})}
for each
{\beta\in\{\mathsf{bf},\mathsf{fb}\}}
; and

(M2)

if
{\alpha\neq{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}
(and thus
{R\neq{\approx}}
), then
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\cup\{\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{u}^{\alpha})\}\models R(\mathbf{u})}
.

The base case holds trivially for each fact in
{I^{0}=B}
. For the induction
step, assume that
I^{i}
satisfies properties
(M1)
and

(M2)
, and consider an arbitrary fact

derived by a rule

and substitution
\sigma
—that is,
{\sigma(\mathsf{b}(r))\subseteq I^{i}}
and

.

Assume
{r\in P}
and consider an arbitrary magic predicate

for

that occurs in the body of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
; moreover, consider arbitrary

{\beta\in\{\mathsf{bf},\mathsf{fb}\}}

in

–

or

be the head of

; thus,
{\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{t}\sigma}
. Also, let

and

information passing to

in

{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\cup\{\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{u}^{\beta})\}\models\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{t}^{\beta})\sigma\wedge R_{1}(\mathbf{t}_{1})\sigma\wedge\dots\wedge R_{j}(\mathbf{t}_{j})\sigma}

otherwise.

), and furthermore assume

\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\cup\{\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(\mathbf{u}^{\mathsf{bf}})\}\models\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1})\sigma\wedge x_{1}\sigma\approx x_{2}\sigma\wedge\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x_{2})\sigma\wedge x_{2}\sigma\approx x_{3}\sigma

Fact
\mathsf{m}_{\approx}^{{\mathsf{b}\hskip 0.56905pt{\wr}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1})\sigma
is entailed trivially because our

{\mathbf{u}^{\mathsf{bf}}=x_{1}\sigma}
. This,
{x_{1}\sigma\neq x_{2}\sigma}
, and the inductive property
(M1)
entail
{x_{1}\sigma\approx x_{2}\sigma}
. Then, the rule added to
\mathcal{R}
in

, which

entail
{x_{2}\sigma\approx x_{3}\sigma}
. Finally, the

) in

ensures that the fact

{x_{1}\sigma\approx x_{3}\sigma}
satisfies property
(M1)
. The

{\beta=\mathsf{fb}}
is analogous because
\mathcal{R}^{*}
contains the symmetry
rule (
4
).

Step 2.
Let
{\alpha=f\cdots f}
. Line
1
of
Algorithm
3
ensures
{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}\leftarrow\;\in\mathcal{R}}

and
{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{D}}
. The former ensures
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}}
. The latter ensures that

\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}
is extracted from
\mathcal{D}
in
line
3
and passed to the
\mathsf{process}
function in
line
10
; thus, line
20
ensures that

\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{Q}}^{\alpha}
occurs in the body of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
. But then, for each
fact of the form
{\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
such that
{P\cup\mathsf{SG}(P)\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, property
(M2)
ensures
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. We now argue that
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
holds as well. In particular, note that
{\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{*}}
, and that
{\mathcal{R}^{*}\setminus\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
consists of zero or more domain
rules of the form (
1
) for a constant
c
not occurring in
\mathcal{R}
,
and domain rules of the form (
2
) for a predicate
R
not occurring
in
\mathcal{R}
. Such rules can derive facts of the form
\mathsf{D}(c)
for a constant
c

occurring in the base instance
B
in a facts with a predicate not occurring in

{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
, which can in turn lead to the derivation of facts of the
form
{c\approx c}
and
{f(\mathbf{u})\approx t}
where
\mathbf{u}
contain such
constant
c
. However, the rules of
\mathcal{R}
not containing the magic predicate in
the head are well-formed, so they cannot apply to such facts in exactly the
same way as in the proof of Theorem
14
. Hence, facts
{c\approx c}
and
{f(\mathbf{u})\approx t}
cannot contribute to the derivation of
the fact
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
21

See
21

Proof.

Let
M
be the maximum depth of an atom in
{T_{P_{1}}^{\infty}(B)}
, and let

{I^{0},I^{1},\dots}
be the sequence of instances used to compute

{T_{P_{2}}^{\infty}(B)}
as defined in Section
2
. We show by
induction on
k
that, for each fact
{F\in I^{k}}
,

•

{\mathsf{dep}(F)\leq M+1}
if
F
contains a magic predicate, and

•

{\mathsf{dep}(F)\leq M}
and
{P_{1}\cup B\models F}
otherwise.

The base case
{I^{0}=B}
is trivial, so we assume that the claim holds for
some
I^{k}
and consider a fact
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{k+1}\setminus I^{k}}

derived by a rule
{r\in P_{2}}
and substitution
\sigma
satisfying

{\mathsf{b}(r)\sigma\subseteq I^{k}}
. We consider the possible forms of the rule

r
.

Assume that
r
was added to
\mathcal{R}
in line
20
. Then,
P_{1}

contains a rule
r^{\prime}
such that
{\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{b}(r)}
, and the
induction assumption ensures
{P_{1}\cup B\models\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\sigma}
. Thus, we
have
{P_{1}\cup B\models F}
and consequently fact
F
is of depth at most
M
.

Assume that
{r\in\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
. Definition
19
ensures that
domain rules (
2
) are not instantiated in
\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})
for the magic
predicates, so
{r\in P_{1}}
and the property holds as in the previous

Assume that

was added to
\mathcal{R}
in line
24
for some

{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
. Let
R(\mathbf{t})
be as in
line
19
, let
{\langle R_{1}(\mathbf{t}_{1}),\dots,R_{n}(\mathbf{t}_{n})\rangle}
be as in line

, and let

be as in line

for

. The
inductive assumption ensures
{\mathsf{dep}(\mathsf{m}_{R}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{t}^{\beta})\sigma)\leq M+1}
and
{\mathsf{dep}(R_{j}(\mathbf{t}_{j})\sigma)\leq M}
for each

with
{1\leq j<i}
. We consider the possible forms of atom
R_{i}(\mathbf{t}_{i})
.

•

Assume predicate
R_{i}
is relational. Since program

is
well-formed, terms
\mathbf{t}_{i}
do not contain a function symbol, so atom

S(\mathbf{t}_{i}^{\gamma_{i}})
does not contain a function symbol either.
Moreover, each variable of

occurs in

or some

with
{1\leq j<i}
due to property (

) of Definition

, so the

. Thus, we have

{\mathsf{dep}(S(\mathbf{t}_{i}^{\gamma_{i}})\sigma)\leq M+1}
, as required.

•

Assume
{R_{i}={\approx}}
so atom

is of the form

{t_{i}^{1}\approx t_{i}^{2}}
. We consider only the case of
{\beta=\mathsf{bf}}

as the case for

is analogous. Property

) of Definition

ensures that, for each

and

holds as well. Consequently, atom

is of depth

{\mathsf{dep}(S(\mathbf{t}_{i}^{\gamma_{i}})\sigma)\leq M+1}
.

Assume that
r
was added to
\mathcal{R}
in
lines

–

. The head of

then
contains a magic predicate; moreover, each term occurring in
\mathsf{h}(r)
is a

\mathsf{b}(r)
, so
{\mathsf{dep}(\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma)\leq M+1}
clearly
holds by the inductive hypothesis.
