Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
29

See
29

Proof.

Let
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
and
P_{3}
–
P_{7}
be as specified in
Algorithm
1
. Program
P_{6}
is safe, and removal of
function symbols ensures that all equalities in the body involve only
variables. Desingularisation is thus applicable to each body equality atom, and
applying it removes the atom while also preserving safety. Moreover, for each
fact of the form
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
, we have
{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{P_{6}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
; this holds by Proposition
3
, Theorem
6
,
Proposition
9
, Theorem
14
,
Theorem
20
, and Proposition
26
. Now consider
arbitrary fact
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Let
{P^{\prime}=\mathsf{sk}(\mathsf{fol}(\Sigma))}
and
{\mathcal{R}^{*}=\mathsf{EQ}(P^{\prime})\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})}
. The properties of equality axiomatisation ensure
{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{P^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P^{\prime})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, and the observation about the relationship between

\Sigma
and
P_{6}
from the previous paragraph ensures
{P^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P^{\prime})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{P_{6}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. The entailment relationship of logic programming then ensures

{P^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P^{\prime})\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if

{P_{6}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
as well;
moreover, for each rule
{r\in\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})}
, there exists a rule
{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{*}}
such that
{\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})=\mathsf{h}(r)}
and
{\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{b}(r)}
, so
\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})
is redundant. All of these observations together
ensure
{\{\Sigma\}\cup B\models_{\approx}\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{P^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
if and only if
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
. Now assume that
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, and let
{I^{0},I^{1},\dots}
be the sequence of
instances used to compute
T_{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})}^{\infty}(B)
as defined in
Section
2
. To prove
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, we show by induction on
i
that
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\cup B\models F}
for each fact
{F\in I^{i}}
. The base case is obvious, so assume
that this property holds for
I^{i}
and consider a fact
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{i+1}\setminus I^{i}}
derived by a rule
{r\in P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})}
and
substitution
\sigma
such that
{\sigma(\mathsf{b}(r))\subseteq I^{i}}
. If
{r\in\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{*}}
, we clearly have
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\models\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma}
. Furthermore, if
{r\in P_{7}}
, then there exists a rule
{r^{\prime}\in P_{6}}
such that
r
is obtained from
r^{\prime}
by desingularisation. Let

\sigma^{\prime}
be the substitution obtained from
\sigma
by setting
{\sigma^{\prime}(x)=t\sigma}
for each variable
x
replaced with term
t
when
Definition
28
is applied to
r
. For each relational body atom

{R(\mathbf{t})\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
, the inductive assumption and
{R(\mathbf{t})\sigma\in I^{i}}
ensure
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\models R(\mathbf{t})\sigma}
; moreover, rule
r^{\prime}

contains a body atom
{R^{\prime}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})}
that satisfies
{R(\mathbf{t})\sigma=R^{\prime}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, as mentioned at the beginning of
this proof, each equality in
\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})
is of the form
{x_{1}\approx x_{2}}
;
moreover,
r^{\prime}
is well-formed, so
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
occur in relational atoms in

\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})
. Also, the definition of
\sigma^{\prime}
ensures
{x_{1}\sigma^{\prime}=x_{2}\sigma^{\prime}}
, so the domain rules (
2
) and the reflexivity rule
(
3
) in
\mathcal{R}^{*}
ensure
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\models x_{1}\sigma^{\prime}\approx x_{2}\sigma^{\prime}}
. Thus, we have
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\models\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime}}
, which
ensures
{P_{6}\cup\mathcal{R}^{*}\models\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime}}
. Finally, the
transformation from Definition
28
ensures
{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma=\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma^{\prime}}
.

(
\Rightarrow
) Assume
{P_{6}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, and
let
{I^{0},I^{1},\dots}
be the sequence of instances used to compute

T_{P_{6}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})}^{\infty}(B)
as defined in
Section
2
. To prove
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models\mathsf{Q}(\mathbf{a})}
, we show by induction on
i
that
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models F}
for each fact
{F\in I^{i}}
. The base case
{I^{0}=B}
is obvious,
so assume that this property holds for
I^{i}
and consider a fact

{\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma\in I^{i+1}\setminus I^{i}}
derived by a rule
{r\in P_{6}\cup\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})}
and substitution
\sigma
such that
{\sigma(\mathsf{b}(r))\subseteq I^{i}}
. First, assume that
{r\in\mathsf{SG}(P_{6})}
. Then there exists a rule
{r^{\prime}\in\mathsf{EQ}(P_{6})=\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})}
such that
{\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})=\mathsf{h}(r)}
and
{\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{b}(r)}
; the only case where
{\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})\subsetneq\mathsf{b}(r)}
is
when
r
is a
\mathsf{D}
-restricted functional reflexivity rule (
22
).
Thus, we have
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma}
. Second,
assume that
{r\in P_{6}}
. The inductive assumption ensures
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models R(\mathbf{t})\sigma}
for each atom
{R(\mathbf{t})\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
. Let

{r^{\prime}\in P_{7}}
be the rule obtained from
r
as specified in
Definition
28
. Now consider an arbitrary relational atom
{A\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
, and let
A^{\prime}
be the corresponding atom in
\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})
. Atom
A^{\prime}
is
obtained from
A
by zero or more replacements of a variable
x
with
t
due
to
{x\approx t\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
. For each such
{x\approx t}
, we have

{x\sigma\approx t\sigma\in I^{i}}
, so the induction assumption ensures
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models x\sigma\approx t\sigma}
. Moreover, we have

{A\sigma\in I^{i}}
, so the induction assumption ensures
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models A\sigma}
. But then, the congruence rules of
\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})
clearly
ensure
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models A^{\prime}\sigma}
. Since this holds for
arbitrary
{A\in\mathsf{b}(r)}
and the corresponding
{A^{\prime}\in\mathsf{b}(r^{\prime})}
, we
have
{P_{7}\cup\mathsf{EQ}(P_{7})\cup B\models\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})\sigma}
. Finally, note that

{\mathsf{h}(r^{\prime})\sigma=\mathsf{h}(r)\sigma}
, which ensures the claim.
∎

Appendix F
Generating Benchmarks for Second-Order Dependencies

In this appendix we discuss our approach for generating second-order
dependencies and datasets. We use the standard notion of a
position
,
which is a possibly empty sequence of integers that identifies an occurrence of
a subexpression in a term or atom. For example, given the atom
R(x,f(y,z),w)
,
the empty position identifies the atom itself; positions
1
,
2
, and
3

identify the variable
x
, the term
f(y,z)
, and the variable
w
,
respectively; position
2.1
identifies the variable
y
; and position
2.2

identifies the variable
z
. Our approach is shown in
Algorithms
4
–
7
, where
Algorithm
4
provides the entry point. It takes a number of
parameters that control the generation process, and it produces a set
\mathcal{R}
of
conjuncts of a generalised SO dependency and a base instance
B
. All elements
of
\mathcal{R}
are of the form (
9
), but with no
existential quantification over individual variables, and with just one atom
per head. In line with the terminology used thus far, we call the elements of

\mathcal{R}

rules
.

Algorithm 4

\mathsf{benchmarkGenerator}

1:

Global:

N_{\mathsf{PA}}

: the maximum predicate arity

N_{\mathsf{FA}}

: the maximum function symbol arity

N_{\mathsf{P}}

: the size of a set of predicates of arity at most
N_{\mathsf{PA}}

N_{\mathsf{FS}}

: the size of a set of function symbols of arity at most
N_{\mathsf{FA}}

N_{\mathsf{C}}

: the size of a set of constants

N_{\mathsf{Q}}

: the number of seed query facts

N_{\mathsf{D}}

: the maximum term depth in derivations considered

N_{\mathsf{R}}

: the maximum number of rules to produce in the first phase

N_{\mathsf{F}}

: the maximum number of rules that derive a certain fact

N_{\mathsf{RA}}

: the maximum number of relational body atoms per rule

N_{\mathsf{EQ}}

: the maximum number of equality body atoms per rule

N_{\mathsf{T}}

: the ‘blowup’ factor used to generate a dataset from the derivation leaves

2:

3:

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset

4:

Set
\Gamma
to the empty graph

5:

\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{\mathsf{Q}_{1}(\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{Q}_{N_{\mathsf{Q}}}(\mathbf{a}_{N_{\mathsf{Q}}})\}
where vectors of constants
\mathbf{a}_{i}
are randomly chosen

6:

while

\mathcal{T}\neq\emptyset

and

|\mathcal{R}|<N_{\mathsf{R}}

do

7:

Choose and remove a fact
F
from
\mathcal{T}

8:

Randomly choose a number
n_{\mathsf{F}}
between one and
N_{\mathsf{F}}

9:

for

i\in\{1,\dots,n_{\mathsf{F}}\}

do

10:

if

F
is an equality fact and this condition is randomly satisfied
then

11:

\overline{\rho}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{createGroundEqualityRule}(F)

12:

else

13:

\langle\rho,\overline{\rho}\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{createRule}(F)

14:

if
the fixpoint of
\mathcal{R}\cup\{\rho\}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R}\cup\{\rho\})
and the critical instance cannot be proved finite
then

15:

continue

16:

else

if
no rule in
\mathcal{R}
subsumes
\rho

then

17:

Remove from
\mathcal{R}
each rule that is subsumed by
\rho

18:

Add
\rho
to
\mathcal{R}

19:

for

each

F_{i}\in\mathsf{b}(\overline{\rho})

do

20:

if

F_{i}\not\in\mathcal{D}

then

21:

Add
F_{i}
to
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{D}

22:

Extend
\Gamma
to contain vertices
F_{i}
and
F
, as well as an edge from
F_{i}
to
F

23:

B\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset

24:

\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\{F\mid F\text{ has zero incoming edges in }\Gamma\}

25:

while

\mathcal{T}\neq\emptyset

do

26:

Choose and remove a fact
S(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
from
\mathcal{T}

27:

if
at least one argument of
S(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
contains a function symbol
then

28:

\langle\rho,\overline{\rho}\rangle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathsf{createTransferRule}(S(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))

29:

Add
\rho
to
\mathcal{R}

30:

Let
R(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
be the (only) atom in
\mathsf{b}(\overline{\rho})

31:

if

R(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\not\in\mathcal{D}

then

32:

Add
R(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
to
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{D}

33:

else

34:

for

each

i\in\{1,\dots,N_{\mathsf{T}}\}

do

35:

Add
S(t_{1}^{(i)},\dots,t_{n}^{(i)})
to
B

36:

return

\langle\mathcal{R},B\rangle

Algorithm 5

\mathsf{createTransferRule}(S(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))

1:

\mathbf{s}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\emptyset

2:

for

each

i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

do

3:

if

t_{i}
is of the form
f(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})

then

4:

Let
\mathbf{x}
be a tuple of
m
fresh variables

5:

Append
f(\mathbf{x})
to
\mathbf{s}
, append
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and append
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}
to
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}

6:

else

7:

Let
x
be a fresh variable

8:

Append
x
to both
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and append
t_{i}
to
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}

9:

Let
R
be a fresh predicate of arity
|\mathbf{s}^{\prime}|

10:

\rho\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=S(\mathbf{s})\leftarrow R(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})
and
\overline{\rho}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=S(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\leftarrow R(\mathbf{t}^{\prime})

11:

return

\langle\rho,\overline{\rho}\rangle

Several steps of the algorithm involve randomly selecting predicates, function
symbols, and constants, as well as generating ground terms. These choices are
governed as follows. To select predicates, the algorithm maintains a cache of
previously selected predicates; then, when a predicate is to be selected, the
algorithm either randomly reuses a predicate from the cache, or it chooses a
new predicate of maximum arity
N_{\mathsf{PA}}
out of a fixed set of
N_{\mathsf{P}}

predicates. Function symbols and constants are handled analogously; the number
and the arity of function symbols are bounded by
N_{\mathsf{FS}}
and

N_{\mathsf{FA}}
, respectively, and the number of constants is bounded by

N_{\mathsf{C}}
. In addition, several steps of the algorithm also involve
generating ground terms of depth at most
d
. To this end, the algorithm
maintains a cache of previously seen terms, and then either randomly reuses a
term from the cache, selects a constant if
d
is zero, or selects a function
symbol and recursively generates arguments of depth at most
d-1
.

The algorithm initialises in line
5
the ‘ToDo’ set

\mathcal{T}
with
N_{\mathsf{Q}}
randomly generated
seed
facts of the
form
\mathsf{Q}_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{i})
. Predicates
\mathsf{Q}_{i}
are all different, while
arguments
\mathbf{a}_{i}
are randomly generated as outlined earlier and are thus
likely to overlap. The algorithm then randomly produces chains of rule
instances that derive the seed facts, and lifts these instances to rules with
variables. It also produces a base instance
B
while ensuring that at least

N_{\mathsf{T}}
copies of each seed fact are derived when
\mathcal{R}
is applied to

B
. Since the arguments of the seed facts overlap, the derivations of the seed
facts are likely to overlap as well. Thus, by using
\mathsf{Q}_{i}
as query
predicates, we can check how well our techniques from
Section
4
identify the inferences relevant to a query. The
algorithm realises this idea in two phases. In the first phase, it generates up
to
N_{\mathsf{R}}
rules matching the form of equation
(
9
), and it also constructs an acyclic graph

\Gamma
that records the derivation dependencies: the vertices of
\Gamma
are
facts occurring in the rule instances, and the edges reflect which facts are
used to produce other facts. In the second phase, the algorithm ‘unfolds’ the
function symbols in the facts occurring in the leaves of
\Gamma
and populates
the base instance
B
. We next describe the two phases in detail.

The first phase comprises
lines
6
–
22
, where the
algorithm processes each fact
F
in the ‘ToDo’ list
\mathcal{T}
and
generates between one and
N_{\mathsf{F}}
ground rules

\overline{\rho}
that derive
F
(i.e., that have
F
in the head). Each

\overline{\rho}
can be either a rule that axiomatises equality
(line
11
) or a conjunct of a generalised SO dependency
(line
13
). In the latter case, a nonground rule
\rho

is also produced such that
\overline{\rho}
is an instance of
\rho
, and the
rule
\rho
is added to
\mathcal{R}
provided that it passes two checks. First, the
algorithm verifies whether the fixpoint of
{\mathcal{R}\cup\{\rho\}}
is finite,
which can be determined using sound but incomplete checks such as
model-summarising or model-faithful acyclicity by

Grau et al. (2013)
. Second, the algorithm checks that

\rho
is not subsumed by another rule in
\mathcal{R}
and is thus not redundant. If
both checks are satisfied, then all rules in
\mathcal{R}
subsumed by
\rho
are
removed in order to keep
\mathcal{R}
free of redundant rules, and
\rho
is added to

\mathcal{R}
. Finally, all facts from the body of the nonground rule
\overline{\rho}

are added to the ‘ToDo’ set
\mathcal{T}
, and set
\mathcal{D}
is used to
make sure that no fact is processed more than once
(lines
19
–
22
). Also,
the graph
\Gamma
is updated to record that the facts in the body of

\overline{\rho}
derive
F
.

The second phase consists of
lines
24
–
35
. It generates the
base instance
B
and the rules that use the facts in
B
to ‘feed’ the rule
instances considered in the first phase. The ‘ToDo’ set
\mathcal{T}
is
initialised to contain all facts occurring in the leaves of
\Gamma
, and the
algorithm next considers each fact
F
in
\mathcal{T}
. If
F
contains no
function symbols, the algorithm adds
N_{\mathsf{T}}
copies of
F
to the
base instance
B
. Otherwise, Algorithm
5
is used to
‘unfold’ top-level function symbols in
F
using so-called ‘transfer’ rules.
Specifically, the latter algorithm produces a ground rule
\overline{\rho}
of
the form
{F\leftarrow F^{\prime}}
such that all functional terms in
F^{\prime}
are of
depth one less than in
F
, as well as a corresponding nonground rule
\rho
.
Fact
F^{\prime}
is then added to the ‘ToDo’ set so that it may be ‘unfolded’ further
or added to the base instance. This is best understood by an example where
F

is
S(a,f(g(b)))
. Algorithm
5
produces a ground
‘transfer’ rule
{S(a,f(g(b)))\leftarrow X(a,g(b))}
and the corresponding
nonground rule
{S(x,f(y))\leftarrow X(x,y)}
. Fact
X(a,g(b))
contains a
function symbol, so it gives rise to a further ‘transfer’ rule
{X(a,g(b))\leftarrow Y(a,b)}
and the corresponding nonground rule
{X(x,g(y))\leftarrow Y(x,y)}
. Finally, fact
Y(a,b)
contains no function symbols, so

N_{\mathsf{T}}
copies of
Y(a,b)
are added to
B
. Predicates
X
and
Y

are fresh—that is, they are not chosen randomly as outlined earlier so that
the inferences by transfer rules do not interact with the inferences of the
rules generated in the first phase.

What remains to be discussed is how the rules deriving
F
in
lines
11
and
13
are produced. For
the former, Algorithm
6
randomly selects a
functional reflexivity rule, a transitivity rule, or a symmetry rule. In the
case of transitivity, the algorithm randomly generates a term
t_{3}
of depth at
most
N_{\mathsf{D}}
. Equality axiomatisation is not meant to be included in
the resulting program
\mathcal{R}
, so Algorithm
6

creates only a ground rule
\overline{\rho}
that derives
F
.

Algorithm 6

\mathsf{createGroundEqualityRule}(t_{1}\approx t_{2})

1:

if

t_{1}=f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
and
t_{2}=f(u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{n}^{\prime})
with
f
a true function symbol, and this condition is randomly satisfied
then

2:

return

f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\approx f(u^{\prime}_{1},\dots,u^{\prime}_{n})\leftarrow u_{1}\approx u_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\dots\wedge u_{n}\approx u_{n}^{\prime}

3:

else

if
this condition is randomly satisfied
then

4:

Let
t_{3}
be a random ground term of depth at most
N_{\mathsf{D}}

5:

return

t_{1}\approx t_{2}\leftarrow t_{1}\approx t_{3}\wedge t_{3}\approx t_{2}

6:

else

7:

return

t_{1}\approx t_{2}\leftarrow t_{2}\approx t_{1}

Algorithm 7

\mathsf{createRule}(F)
where
F=R_{0}(t_{1},\dots,t_{a_{0}})
and
a_{0}
is the arity of
R_{0}

1:

Set
G
to the empty graph, and randomly choose numbers
n_{\mathsf{RA}}
and
n_{\mathsf{EQ}}
between one and
N_{\mathsf{RA}}
and
N_{\mathsf{EQ}}
, respectively

2:

for

each

{j\in\{1,\dots,a_{0}\}}

do

3:

if

t_{j}
is of the form
f(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})
, and either the depth of
t_{j}
is
N_{\mathsf{D}}+1
or this branch is chosen randomly
then

4:

F_{0,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=f

5:

for

each

k\in\{1,\dots,m\}

do

6:

Add the
head position
vertex
v=\langle 0,j.k\rangle
to
G
, let
u_{v}
be a fresh variable, and let
\overline{u}_{v}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=s_{k}

7:

else

8:

F_{0,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bot

9:

Add the
head position
vertex
v=\langle 0,j\rangle
to
G
, let
u_{v}
be a fresh variable, and let
\overline{u}_{v}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=t_{j}

10:

for

each

i\in\{1,\dots,n_{\mathsf{RA}}\}

do

11:

Set
R_{i}
to a randomly chosen predicate

12:

for

each

j\in\{1,\dots,a_{i}\}
where
a_{i}
is the arity of
R_{i}

do

13:

F_{i,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bot

14:

Add the
relational body position
vertex
\langle i,j\rangle
to
G

15:

for

each

i\in\{n_{\mathsf{RA}}+1,\dots,n_{\mathsf{RA}}+n_{\mathsf{EQ}}\}

do

16:

R_{i}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}={\approx}
and
a_{i}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=2

17:

for

each

j\in\{1,2\}

do

18:

if
this condition is randomly satisfied
then

19:

F_{i,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\bot

20:

Add the
equality body position
vertex
\langle i,j\rangle
to
G

21:

else

22:

Set
F_{i,j}
to a randomly chosen function symbol

23:

for

each

k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
where
m
is the arity of
F_{i,j}

do

24:

Add the
equality body position
vertex
\langle i,j.k\rangle
to
G

25:

Randomly extend
G
with edges such that (i) graph
G
is acyclic, (ii) each head position vertex has zero incoming edges

26:

and at least one outgoing edge to a relational body position vertex, (iii) each relational body position vertex has at

27:

most one incoming edge, and (iv) each equality body position vertex has one incoming edge and no outgoing edges.

28:

for

each
relational body position vertex
v
with no incoming edges
do

29:

Let
u_{v}
be a fresh variable

30:

Let
\overline{u}_{v}
be a random ground term of depth at most
N_{\mathsf{D}}

31:

while

G
contains a vertex
v
such that
u_{v}
and
\overline{u}_{v}
are undefined
do

32:

Choose a vertex
v
in
G
such that
u_{v}
and
\overline{u}_{v}
are undefined

33:

Choose a vertex
v^{\prime}
in
G
such that
u_{v^{\prime}}
and
\overline{u}_{v^{\prime}}
are defined and
G
contains an edge from
v^{\prime}
to
v

34:

{u_{v}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=u_{v^{\prime}}}
and
{\overline{u}_{v}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\overline{u}_{v^{\prime}}}

35:

for

each

{i\in\{0,\dots,n_{\mathsf{RA}}+n_{\mathsf{EQ}}\}}
and
each

{j\in\{1,\dots,a_{i}\}}

do

36:

if

F_{i,j}=\bot

then

37:

Let
v
be the vertex of
G
of the form
\langle i,j\rangle

38:

w_{i,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=u_{v}
and
\overline{w}_{i,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=\overline{u}_{v}

39:

else

40:

Let
m
be the arity of
F_{i,j}
and let
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
be all vertices of
G
of the form
\langle i,j.k\rangle
for
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}

41:

w_{i,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=F_{i,j}(u_{v_{1}},\dots,u_{v_{m}})
and
\overline{w}_{i,j}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=F_{i,j}(\overline{u}_{v_{1}},\dots,\overline{u}_{v_{m}})

42:

\rho\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=R_{0}(w_{0,1},\dots,w_{0,a_{0}})\leftarrow\bigwedge\limits_{i=1}^{n_{\mathsf{RA}}+n_{\mathsf{EQ}}}R_{i}(w_{i,1},\dots,w_{i,a_{i}})

43:

\overline{\rho}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.03334pt}{$\cdot$}}=R_{0}(\overline{w}_{0,1},\dots,\overline{w}_{0,a_{0}})\leftarrow\bigwedge\limits_{i=1}^{n_{\mathsf{RA}}+n_{\mathsf{EQ}}}R_{i}(\overline{w}_{i,1},\dots,\overline{w}_{i,a_{i}})

44:

return

\langle\rho,\overline{\rho}\rangle

Algorithm
7
creates a nonground rule
\rho
that may be
added to
\mathcal{R}
and the corresponding instance
\overline{\rho}
. Roughly
speaking, this is done by generating a graph
G
containing a vertex of the
form
{\langle i,p\rangle}
for each head or body atom of
\rho
and each
position
p
in the atom: vertices of the form
{\langle 0,p\rangle}

identify positions in the head of
\rho
, and vertices of the form
{\langle i,p\rangle}
with
{i\geq 1}
identify positions in the
i
-th body atom of

\rho
. The edges of
G
will describe the joins among the positions of the
atoms of
\rho
and
\overline{\rho}
. Each vertex
v
in
G
will be
associated with a variable
u_{v}
and a ground term
\overline{u}_{v}
. Finally, for
each argument
j
of an atom
i
, the algorithm will define
F_{i,j}
as either
a function symbol or a special symbol
\bot
. Intuitively,
{F_{i,j}\neq\bot}
means that the
j
-th argument of the
i
-th atom of
\rho
and

\overline{\rho}
are
{F_{i,j}(u_{\langle i,j.1\rangle},\dots,u_{\langle i,j.m\rangle})}
and
{F_{i,j}(\overline{u}_{\langle i,j.1\rangle},\dots,\overline{u}_{\langle i,j.m\rangle})}
, respectively; in contrast,
{F_{i,j}=\bot}
means that the
j
-th argument of the
i
-th atom of
\rho
and

\overline{\rho}
are
{u_{\langle i,j\rangle}}
and
{\overline{u}_{\langle i,j\rangle}}
, respectively. Thus,
G
,
F_{i,j}
,
u_{v}
, and
\overline{u}_{v}

correctly describe the arguments of all atoms in
\rho
and
\overline{\rho}
,
and the main objective of Algorithm
7
is to randomly select
these values while avoiding any contradictions.

To this end, Algorithm
7
analyses each argument
t_{j}
of
{F=\mathsf{h}(\overline{\rho})}
. If
t_{j}
is a functional term involving a function
symbol
f
, the algorithm can choose whether the
j
-th argument of

\mathsf{h}(\rho)
should be a term of the form
f(\mathbf{x})
or a variable. In the
former case
(lines
4
–
6
),

F_{0,j}
is set to
f
, a vertex
{v=\langle 0,j.k\rangle}
is introduced
for each argument
k
of
f
, a fresh variable
u_{v}
is introduced for
each such
v
, and
\overline{u}_{v}
is set to the
k
-th argument of
t_{j}
. In the
latter case
(lines
8
–
9
), only
vertex
{v=\langle 0,j\rangle}
is introduced,
F_{0,j}
is set to
\bot
,
a fresh variable
u_{v}
is introduced for the
j
-th argument of

\mathsf{h}(\rho)
, and
\overline{u}_{v}
is set to
t_{j}
. If
t_{j}
does not contain a
function symbol (i.e., if
t_{j}
is a constant), then only the latter case is
possible.

In lines
10
–
14

and
15
–
24
, the algorithm
creates the vertices of
G
that correspond to the relational and equality body
atoms, respectively, of
\rho
and
\overline{\rho}
. Relational atoms contain
variable arguments only (cf. Section
2
), so only
vertices of the form
{\langle i,j\rangle}
are added for relational atoms. In
contrast, equality atoms can contain functional terms, so for each argument of
an equality atom the algorithm chooses whether to introduce a functional term.
These steps do
not
yet determine
u_{v}
and
\overline{u}_{v}
.

Graph
G
is next extended in line
27
with edges that
encode the propagation of terms among the positions of the body atoms of
\rho

and
\overline{\rho}
. In
lines
28
–
30
, for
each ‘leaf’ vertex
v
(i.e., for each vertex with no incoming edges),

u_{v}
is assigned a fresh variable, and
\overline{u}_{v}
is assigned a random
ground term of depth at most
N_{\mathsf{D}}
. These choices are next propagated
via the edges of
G
to all the remaining terms of
G
in
lines
31
–
34
. To avoid
contradictions, graph
G
must satisfy the properties in
line
27
: it must be acyclic; the head positions cannot have
an incoming edge because their values have already been determined, and the
corresponding variables must occur in relational body positions to satisfy the
safety requirement; relational body positions can receive at most one value
from another position; and equality body positions must receive exactly one
value and should not propagate it further to satisfy the safety requirement.
Thus, the structure of the rule has been determined after
line
34
: if vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
satisfy

{u_{v_{1}}=u_{v_{2}}}
, then the atom positions corresponding to
v_{1}
and

v_{2}
participate in a join.
Lines
35
–
41
then
construct a nonground term
w_{i,j}
and a ground term
\overline{w}_{i,j}
for the

j
-th argument of the
i
-th atom of
\rho
and
\overline{\rho}
using

F_{i,j}
and the corresponding vertices of
G
. Finally, the rules
\rho

and
\overline{\rho}
are constructed in lines
42

and
43
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{R}
and
B
be the output of Algorithm
4
. Clearly, when
computing the fixpoint of
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
on
B
, all rule instances
considered during the generation process will derive copies of the considered
facts; however, the rules
\rho
considered in lines
13

and
28
can give rise to other rule instances too.
Thus, computing the fixpoint of
{\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{SG}(\mathcal{R})}
on
B
considers at least
the rule instances produced during benchmark generation (but it often considers
additional rule instances as well). This, in turn, ensures that the benchmarks
provide nontrivial workloads for the techniques from
Section
4
.
